Two defects, not one: the crash, and that `ValueError` is not in `USER_FACING_ERRORS`, so the documented `{"status": "ERROR"}` stderr contract was broken as well. `int` was also quietly wrong where it did not crash — it accepted the string `"12"` and truncated `True` to `1`. Strict rather than forgiving because these two render side by side into `report.md`: a guessed timestamp appears as `[27:46:39–27:46:39]` next to a working YouTube deep link, which is exactly the invented position the transcript panel already refuses (D-069). A new error type rather than reusing `PipelineError`: `io` imports nothing from the package, and that is what lets everything import it |
| D-075 | `io.read_json` rejects a numeric literal that *parses* to an infinity, not merely the `NaN`/`Infinity` tokens | accepted | `parse_constant` only ever sees the bare tokens, so `1e999` — legal JSON — passed the reader and every validator, then died inside `artifacts._write_group` with `Out of range float values are not JSON compliant: inf` and a full traceback. The read succeeded and the write could not, which is the worst arrangement: the damage was already committed to the document by the time anything objected. `read_json`'s docstring promised "canonical JSON must be readable everywhere"; the check simply belonged on the parsed number rather than on its spelling, so it moved to `parse_float` |
| D-076 | A cue timing line is one whose **left side** is a timestamp (`_CUE_TIMING`), not one that merely contains `-->` | accepted | `_cue_chunks` located cues with `if "-->" in line`, so a caption reading `The mapping A --> B is important.` was taken for a timing line and `parse_timestamp` rejected the **entire file** with `Invalid timestamp: 'The'`. The module had already solved this exact class of problem once — `_TIMED_TEXT` restricts its groups to timestamp shapes so that `[Applause - laughter]` is read as text — and the same guard was missing here. Only the left side is anchored, on purpose: a line that really is a cue timing but has a damaged *end* must still reach `parse_timestamp` and be reported, rather than silently degrading into body text and losing a cue. `_TIMESTAMP_TOKEN` is the parser's own grammar without the capture names, and a test asserts the two accept the same timestamps |
| D-077 | One implementation of "where does the transcript end" (`transcripts.transcript_end_sec`), and one rule for reading a canonical file (it must be a JSON object) | accepted | `max((c.get("end_sec", 0) for c in captions), default=0)` existed **four times** — in `transcript_integrity`, `validators.validate_provenance`, `pipeline.validate_run` and `artifacts.apply_extraction_bundle` — at three different guard levels. The loose copies raised `AttributeError` on a caption that is a string and `TypeError` on one whose `end_sec` is `null`, because `max` then compares `None` with a float; both escaped `validate` and `finalize` as raw tracebacks. In the same family, `pipeline._read_canonical` enforced "must be a JSON object" and its twin `artifacts._read` did not — the `io.JsonReadError` docstring says three readers were consolidated into one, and two divergent *wrappers* were left behind — so a `metadata.json` holding `[]` reached `metadata["video_id"]` as `TypeError: list indices must be integers`, and `apply-bundle` read the id raw where `finalize_run` already read it through `_checked_video_id`. The helper is deliberately **tolerant** and the only tolerant thing here: it answers a question for callers whose job is to go on and *report* the damage, and whether a damaged caption is an error stays `transcript_integrity`'s single decision (new codes `caption_not_object`, `captions_not_array`) |
| D-078 | A run directory or `library/` with no project-relative form is **skipped and named**, keyed by where the scan found it (`scanner._canonical_key`) | accepted | `project_relative` calls `.resolve()`, so a run directory that is a *symlink* to somewhere outside the project resolves outside it and the call raises `AdapterError`. It was the **first statement** of `_examine` — outside the `try:` that implements the D-043 skip-and-name contract — and it appeared a second time inside `_apply`'s prior-row lookup. One symlinked directory under `output/` therefore took the whole index down even with `strict=False`: the scan left `state='error'` with the previous counts still in the row, and every endpoint answered `503` for **every** run. `run_dirs` globs `*/metadata.json` and `glob` follows directory symlinks, so an ordinary "runs live on an external drive" setup reaches it. The key is computed **lexically** from the discovered path, so a symlink's target cannot change which row a run owns, and the key is computed once and passed in — removing the second, unguarded call site rather than wrapping it. The reason names the project-relative key **only**: the resolved target is a host path and `skipped_runs[].reason` is served by `/api/status` (D-030, ADR-0003). `strict=True` still raises, because `adapt_project` raises and `T-104`'s equivalence needs the two to agree record for record |
| D-079 | Every `usePaged` request belongs to one **generation** of the query; a page from a retired generation is dropped whole | accepted | The effect aborted only its own controller. `loadMore` created a controller nobody ever aborted and its `.then` had no liveness check, so switching query while a "More" page was in flight let the stale page append onto the new query's items **and** overwrite `next` with the old query's cursor — the following "More" then paginated a collection the header no longer named, silently, with no error the reader could see. The generation is bumped in the effect's cleanup, which React runs before the next effect and on unmount, so a page already in flight can tell that its query is no longer the one on screen. Dropped **whole** rather than partially applied, because `items` and `next` only mean anything together. Two smaller things fell out of it: the `.finally` was an unconditional `setState` that ran after unmount, and the effect now clears `loadingMore` — without which the spinner could stay on forever once the stale `loadMore` that owned it stopped clearing it |
| D-080 | `VirtualList`'s `scrollToIndex` is a request held until the reader scrolls somewhere the component did not put them | accepted | Answering the request takes more than one pass — `offsets[scrollToIndex]` is only an estimate until the rows above the target have been on screen and measured — so the effect has to depend on `offsets`. What it must not do is keep re-applying for the life of the list, and it did: `offsets` is a `useMemo` keyed on `version`, `measure` bumps `version` on every row whose height changes, so a reader who scrolled away from a deep-linked caption was dragged back to it each time a row reported its height. `pending` holds the request and `enforced` distinguishes the reader's scroll from the event the component's own assignment provokes. The file's header comment already named why no test could see this — jsdom reports `offsetHeight === 0`, so `measure` never bumped `version` — and the tests now **stub `offsetHeight`** to drive real measurement rather than working around its absence, which is the part that closes the blind spot rather than the bug alone |
| D-081 | `schemas/extraction_bundle.schema.json` accepts `audit_attempts: 0`, and the test named after that check actually runs `jsonschema` | accepted | The schema bounded `audit_attempts` at `minimum: 1` while `coverage.create_pending_coverage` writes `0` into every fresh run — so the pipeline produced a document its own schema rejected. Three artifacts blessed `0` as the honest never-audited state (`validate_coverage`, WORKFLOW.md §4.4, `prompts/05`) and the schema was the outlier, so the schema moved. What let it survive is the more useful finding: `test_a_fresh_run_writes_windows_the_bundle_schema_accepts` carried that name while asserting only that the statuses written are in `COVERAGE_STATUSES` — it never validated the document against the schema at all. **A test named after a check must perform it**; it now does, and a second test asserts the schema's `maximum` equals `constants.MAX_AUDIT_ATTEMPTS` so the bound stated in two places cannot drift |
| D-082 | `finalize_run` refuses a failing run with `pipeline.VerdictRefusal`, which carries the verdict; `cli.main` catches it before `USER_FACING_ERRORS` and exits through `VERDICT_EXIT_CODES` | accepted | The same run, read from the same `validation.json`, exited `4` through `validate` and `1` through `finalize` — and `1` is documented as "the command refused or failed — a bad argument, a missing or corrupt canonical file, … a broken install", which is what a wrapper reads it as. `VERDICT_EXIT_CODES` exists precisely so the three commands cannot drift into disagreeing, and the refusal path went around it. The error carries the status rather than the refusal site restating the mapping, so there is still exactly one table. The stderr envelope now says `"status": "FAIL"` instead of `"ERROR"`: a verdict is a result to act on, and calling it an error is the same category mistake as the exit code. `VerdictRefusal` subclasses `PipelineError`, so every existing `except` keeps working |
| D-083 | A `Range` header's digits are converted by `media._byte_count`, which answers `size + 1` for a number too long to be an offset | accepted | CPython refuses to convert a decimal string of more than 4300 digits and raises `ValueError`, which nothing in `media.py` caught — so `Range: bytes=<5000 nines>-` answered an undeclared `500` to any unauthenticated client while 4299 digits answered `206` correctly. The route declares `200, 206, 400, 404, 416, 503` and nothing else, so this was contract drift reachable without credentials. **Not** a try/except around the conversion: a number that cannot be an offset into a file of `size` bytes does not need converting exactly — it is simply past the end, which the satisfiability checks already handle correctly (a start past the end is `416`; an end past it is clamped; a suffix larger than the file is the whole file, per RFC 9110). Leading zeros are stripped first so padding is not mistaken for magnitude, and no conversion of more than a few characters is ever attempted |
| D-084 | The frozen API contract ships **inside** the package as `src/x2knwldg/server/openapi.json`, served from beside the module that serves it | accepted | `_FROZEN_SPEC` was `Path(__file__).resolve().parents[3] / "schemas" / …`, documented as "relative to the installed package" and in fact relative to a repo checkout — and with no `[tool.setuptools.package-data]` and no `MANIFEST.in`, no wheel carried the file at all, so `GET /api/openapi.json` was permanently `404 {"detail": "spec not packaged"}` in every installed package. CI could not see it: the `ui` job installs with `-e`, and the non-editable job has no fastapi and never touches the route — so the test that proves it now **builds a wheel and looks inside**, because nothing short of that is the claim. `schemas/api/v1/openapi.json` stays the authored contract that the generator and every schema test read; this is a committed copy, guarded by a byte-identity test exactly as the generated `types.d.ts` is. A duplicate file is the cost; the alternative was making the authored home a derived one |
| D-085 | One host-path scrubber, `io.scrub_host_paths`, used by `scanner`'s recorded reasons, the scan's failure message, and `mcp_server._redact` | accepted | `_project_relative_reason` was a single `reason.replace(str(run_dir), canonical_dir)`, so it redacted only paths *under* the run directory — while `project_relative`'s own message also names the absolute project root, and a symlink names a path outside the run entirely. Every `project_relative` failure therefore named the operator's checkout in `skipped_runs[].reason`, which D-050 put on `/api/status`; and the scan's failure message was stored as `f"{type(exc).__name__}: {exc}"` and served verbatim in a `503` body. D-030 and ADR-0003 both forbid a host path in an error body, and an enumeration of the paths that might appear was never going to be complete — so there are two stages: the caller's meaningful substitutions, then a catch-all that reduces anything still absolute to its last segment. **D-063 is why it scrubs rather than truncates:** the reason still has to state the damage, because deleting the sentence would satisfy a bare no-host-path assertion and silently close D-045's diagnostic channel. Surfacing the library reason (D-087) would have created a fresh instance of this leak, and the test written for D-087 caught it |
| D-086 | A scan that fails over a `ready` index leaves it **`ready`**, with `index.message` saying the last scan failed and was rolled back | accepted | `_apply` performs every write inside one `with connection:`, so a failure rolls all of them back and the stored records are exactly what they were — the existing test asserted that much, and its name already claimed the scan "leaves the previous index". It then committed `state='error'` anyway, and `_require_ready` refuses *every* endpoint in that state, so the records being intact bought the reader nothing: adding one run that duplicated an existing `video_id` cost them every previously-indexed run until the cause was removed. `built_at` is preserved, so the index does not claim to be fresher than it is. This needed a new reporting channel or it would have traded a loud failure for a silent one: `index.message` is **additive and optional** in the frozen contract (`minLength: 1`, absent rather than `null` when there is nothing to say), which is the D-028/R18 discipline — generalize additively, do not rewrite. An index that was never `ready` still reports `error`, because there is nothing there to spare |
| D-087 | `/api/status` reports `runs.library_skipped_reason` — a field of the fragment's own, not an entry in `skipped` | accepted | `_runs_seen` filters the `library/` row out of `discovered` and `indexed` for good reasons already recorded there, and it filtered it out of `skipped` as well — so the `library_skipped_reason` the scanner *did* record surfaced nowhere. A broken `library/concepts.json` and a deleted `library/` both read as 6 entities, 6 relations, `skipped: []` and 0 library nodes against a healthy 7/9/1: D-043's silent zero, on the one endpoint that exists to be honest, with `test_api_honest_status.py` never damaging the library. A field rather than a list entry because the frozen `runs` description states `discovered == indexed + len(skipped)` and appending would have falsified it — and because `ScanReport.library_skipped_reason` already decided this reason deserves somewhere a caller can test "without matching on a path". **Absent for an absent `library/`**: that is the ordinary state of a project that has not run `rebuild-library`, and only a present unreadable fragment is damage — which is what makes the two distinguishable at last |
| D-088 | A `whole` build discards the search corpus along with every other table, hook or no hook | accepted | `build_index`'s docstring promises it discards "whatever was stored", and the loop discarded every record family *except* `documents`, `document_tokens` and `documents_trigrams` — left to `index_documents`, a parameter defaulting to `None`. So `build_index(root)`, the default signature, rebuilt the records and left the previous pass's corpus: measured, an edited unit's new text was unfindable while its deleted text was still being returned, with `total` counting it. The CLI wires the hook; nothing forced it. This is **not** the "half-populating" the existing note rules out, and it needs none of `search`'s per-source logic: `delete-all` is FTS5's own bulk command for retiring an external-content index, so no old text has to be read back and the scanner gains no dependency on the module that fills the tables. Clearing happens before the hook, so a hooked build is unaffected |
| D-089 | `import_transcript` writes the same `validation.json` shape `validate_run` does, verdict included, from the shared `_run_verdict` | accepted | It wrote the five sections and **no top-level `status`** and no `provenance` section, so `adapters.base.read_status` — which reads the top-level `status` — reported `overall: UNKNOWN` for every imported run: a run the pipeline had just validated, describing itself as unchecked, until somebody happened to run `validate`. A fresh import is an honest `PARTIAL`: every validator passes over an empty unit set and `coverage.json` says `PARTIAL` about itself because nothing has been audited, with `audit_attempts: 0` still there to tell a scaffolded `PARTIAL` from an audited one. The verdict rule was extracted rather than copied, because two writers of one file disagreeing about what its sections mean is how this defect started. `test_a_scaffolded_run_produces_a_valid_source_record` asserted the `UNKNOWN`; its real guarantee — a scaffolded run is never a `PASS` — is now asserted directly |
| D-090 | `io.write_group` is the one multi-file write, it takes a `prune` list of directories the group owns, and `import_transcript` uses it | accepted | Two failures with one cause. **Nothing was ever removed:** apply a bundle with KU-001 and KU-002, finalize, retract KU-002, finalize again — `report.md` drops it and `vault/knowledge_units/source/KU-002.md` is still there, linked from nothing and indistinguishable from a unit that still exists, with no validator looking at the vault. **And the run was not written atomically:** `import_transcript` wrote eleven files one at a time, so a failure part way through left a directory that could be neither validated (`Missing JSON file: segments.json`) nor re-imported, because `RunAlreadyExists` keys on `transcript.json` and that was the second file written — only a manual `rm -rf` recovered. `_write_group` was the existing mechanism for exactly this and was unreachable from `pipeline`, which `artifacts` imports; moving it to `io`, which imports nothing from the package, is what lets both reach it. `prune` names the three subtrees `_obsidian_files` generates into rather than `vault/`, so a note a reader put elsewhere under the vault is not something finalize deletes. `report.md` and `raw/transcript.md` join the group, which also stops them being written through a text-mode handle whose `newline=None` rewrites every `\n` as `os.linesep` |
| D-091 | `mcp_server._boundary` redacts the **reply**, not only the failures | accepted | `_redact` was called exclusively inside the `except` arms, so a successful return was passed through verbatim — violating this module's own rule 2, "absolute paths never appear in a successful reply either". Measured: `rebuild_cross_video_library` returned `skipped_runs[].reason` and `path` naming the operator's checkout, and `finalize_video` returned `report` and `graph` as absolute paths. The one test that covered this covered exactly *one* tool, which is why the leak survived in the others. Fixed at this boundary rather than at the producers, because the producers are not wrong: the CLI prints those paths to a user standing on that machine, and it is the MCP surface that has the constraint. **Two rules, not one:** a reply carries extracted knowledge and an error message does not, so the named roots are substituted everywhere — they can never be meaningful content — while the catch-all applies only to a string that is *entirely* a path, which is what a `path`/`report`/`graph` field is. A unit quoting `/usr/local/bin/foo` mid-sentence therefore keeps its text |
| D-092 | The omission-item keys `type` and `note` are stated in `prompts/05` and in the bundle schema, and a committed fixture carries them | accepted | `validate_coverage` reads `omission["type"]` and `omission["note"]`; `prompts/05` named neither, and the schema typed the items as a bare `{"type": "object"}` with no properties — so a model emitting `{"reason": "sponsor"}` passed the schema and was then rejected by `apply-bundle` as `invalid_omission_reason` naming `null`. The keys appeared only in `validators.py` and an archival spec the workflow never links. Compounding it, **all three committed fixtures carried `omitted_items: []`**, so this path was unreachable in CI and only the gitignored real sample ever exercised it. All three artifacts now say the same thing, and a test compares the schema and the validator case by case — which immediately found a fourth disagreement: `validate_coverage` tests `str(note).strip()` while the schema's `minLength: 1` accepted `"   "`, so the note now carries `pattern: "\\S"`. The fixture's omissions go on a **covered** window, so the change costs no status and no window boundary |
| D-093 | `captionIndexAt`'s fallback is bounded by the end of the transcript: past the end is `null`, a gap is still the caption that was playing | accepted | The fallback was never bounded, so on a 3-caption transcript ending at 15s `captionIndexAt(caps, 999999)` returned `2` — `?t=999999` set `aria-current="location"` on the final caption and scrolled to it, and `reader.transcript.noCaptionAt` never fired. `TranscriptPanel` documents the opposite in its own header, and a rendered position no data supports is the one thing this project does not do. The distinction that makes both behaviours right is whether a **later caption exists**: with one, the offset is inside the medium and in a gap, which is what the fallback is for; without one, `fallback` is the last positioned caption and its end is the end of the transcript. A caption stating no end time bounds nothing and stays the honest answer. The test that asserted the old behaviour had a name and a comment that both described the new one |
| D-094 | `VirtualList` rebuilds its measured heights when `estimateHeight` changes, and `offsets` depends on the estimate and on `items` by identity | accepted | The rebuild was keyed on `items.length` alone, so a changed estimate was ignored for the life of the list: five rows at 44px re-rendered as five rows at 99px kept a 220px runway instead of 495px, because every entry was already filled at the old estimate and no length had changed. In-app trigger: navigating between two transcripts with equal caption counts. An entry still sitting at the *old* estimate was never measured and takes the new one; a real measurement beats either estimate and is carried over. The `useMemo` deps had to move too — with `items.length` alone the rebuilt array was never re-read, which is the kind of half-fix that leaves a test passing against the wrong mechanism |
| D-095 | `ReaderView` seeds its `seek` state from the linked offset, so a deep link reaches the player as well as the transcript | accepted | D-069 carried a hit's offset into the Reader and this dropped it: `linkedSeconds` went only to `TranscriptPanel`'s `highlightSec`, so `?tab=transcript&t=300` highlighted the right caption and then "Load player" started the embed at `0` — while `embedUrl` had supported `start` all along. Only the external YouTube link preserved the offset, which answers canvas plan §17.3 scenario 2 by *leaving the application*. Seeding the request is the whole fix: `MediaPanel` already turns a request made before the player exists into the frame's `start`, and one made after it into a `postMessage` seek. A malformed or negative `t` is still **ignored, never coerced** (D-069), which the tests assert in both directions. `ReaderView` had no test file at all before this |
| D-096 | §3's "measured from disk" table is guarded by tests that measure: file counts, the `ui`-is-a-stub claim, and the header date against the rows | accepted | An external audit found four rows stale at once — 1263 lines / 398 tests / seven API test files against 1457 / 600 / eleven, and 50 files / ~6.2k lines / 125 tests in 14 files for the frontend — plus `T-008: ui is a refusing stub` for a command `T-116` had shipped, in four documents. The header read "Measured from disk on 2026-08-31" while carrying rows dated 2026-09-02, so freshness could not be read off it either. Line counts and test totals move on almost every commit and stay §7.3's business; what is asserted is the countable structure that drifts **silently** — how many files there are — because a wrong file count looks exactly like a right one. The header date is asserted against the newest date anywhere in the board, which is the specific inconsistency that made the table unreadable |
| D-097 | `coverage.create_pending_coverage` computes a non-final window's end as `(index + 1) * window_sec`, with no `min` | accepted | The `min(duration, …)` could never bind: `window_count = ceil(duration / window_sec)`, so for every index below the last, `(index + 1) * window_sec` is strictly less than `duration` by construction. A guard that cannot fire reads as a bound the next reader has to reason about, and there is exactly one real edge here — the final window, which owns whatever remains and is handled on the other branch. The property the dead `min` looked like it was protecting is now asserted directly: no window reaches past the duration, and the windows are contiguous |
| D-098 | The segmenter skips a segment whose captions are a **subset** of the previous one's and whose text is empty | accepted | A non-speech cue long enough to reach `max_sec` on its own made the walk emit a segment that was the tail of the one just emitted, with no text — an input segment with nothing in it to extract from, handed to passes 1 and 4 as though it held content. Zero occurrences over the 500 realistic transcripts the audit fuzzed, which is why it is debt and not a defect. **Skipped, not merged, and only when it is a subset**: those captions are already carried by the previous segment, so nothing is lost, which is what keeps the no-caption-loss invariant true. A text-empty segment covering captions no other segment does is still emitted, because dropping *that* would lose them — a transcript of pure silence still gets its segment |
| D-099 | `youtube.fetch_metadata` names why it could not fetch, and `metadata.json` carries it as `metadata_error` | accepted | `except Exception: return {}` collapsed a network failure, a geo-block, an age gate, a bot check and a yt-dlp API change into one answer — "no title, no channel, no language" — leaving three silent `None`s and no way to tell which had happened. The metadata is genuinely optional (a transcript is what this project needs), but *why* it is absent is not, which is D-045's rule applied to the one fetch that had escaped it. The absent-extra case is named too: "the extra is not installed" and "the fetch was blocked" both produce no title, and only one is fixed by installing something. Still a broad `except`, on purpose — yt-dlp raises its own hierarchy plus whatever its HTTP stack does, and the caller's contract is "carry on without the metadata". What changed is that carrying on now says so, and the message is scrubbed because it can carry a path |
| D-100 | An artifact whose path has no project-relative form is reported in `adapter_metadata.unmappable_artifacts`; a run's digest excludes symlinks | accepted | `Adapter.relative` resolves symlinks, so a canonical file that *is* a symlink to outside the project resolved outside it and raised — measured, a symlinked `report.md`, `raw/source.srt` or vault note each made `adapt_run` refuse the **whole run**. Since D-078 that is skipped-and-named rather than a dead index, which is better and still wrong: one file should not cost a run. `unmappable_artifacts` is the channel that already exists for a thing the index cannot address (D-045). **Not** mapped to its lexical path instead: the bytes really do live outside the project, `media.py`'s containment check would refuse to serve them, and an artifact in the index whose bytes cannot be fetched is a promise the API does not keep. Separately, `_run_files` used `path.is_file()`, which follows links — so the *target* went into the digest and `io.sha256_file` read it in full on every scan, for a file the run does not own, whose changes moved the digest and whose repointing at identical bytes did not |
| D-101 | `constants.MAX_PAGE_LIMIT` is the one page bound, honoured by the HTTP surface **and** the MCP tool | accepted | `server/params.py` capped a page at 500 and the MCP tool capped nothing: `query.search_knowledge` floor-checks `limit` and says in as many words that it has no ceiling on purpose, because a local CLI search is bounded by the corpus. That reasoning is right for the CLI and wrong for a tool an agent calls — `limit=10**18` returned the entire corpus in one reply. Two bounds for the same data was the defect, so the bound is stated once and both remote surfaces read it. **Refused rather than clamped**: a silently reduced page is a different answer to the one asked for (D-020), and the CLI keeps its uncapped local search |
| D-102 | `prepare_inbox` writes its note through `io.write_text` | accepted | It used `Path.write_text`, whose `newline=None` rewrites every `\n` as `os.linesep` and which is not atomic — invisible on macOS, certain on Windows. `raw/transcript.md` and `report.md` were the other two bypasses and joined `write_group` with D-090; this was the last one |
| D-103 | `create_app` installs a `Host` allowlist, defaulting to the loopback names and never `*` | accepted | There was no `Host` validation at all. The bind is correctly loopback-only (ADR 0001 invariant 9) and there is no CORS middleware, so a page on another origin cannot *read* a reply — but **DNS rebinding does not need CORS**: a name the attacker controls, resolved to `127.0.0.1`, makes their page same-origin with this server, and every route is a readable `GET` over the whole knowledge base. Binding to loopback stops other machines, not other origins on this one. An allowlist rather than a check that the name resolves to loopback, because the resolution is the attacker's to control and the set of names this server is reachable at is small and knowable. Installed before the routers, so a rebound name is refused before a route reads the index. The tests point `TestClient` at `http://127.0.0.1` rather than adding its default `testserver` to the allowlist: a test-only name in a production default is how an allowlist stops being one |
| D-104 | `routes/media.py` checks a stated `media_type` before it becomes a `Content-Type` header | accepted | The value went into the header with no validation. Outside latin-1 (`"tëxt/plåin"`) it failed header encoding and answered an undeclared `500`; containing CRLF it was placed in the header dict unchecked, with only h11's wire-level refusal between that and a split response. It needs index write access, which is why it is low — but the route performed no validation of its own, and "something downstream refuses it" is not a check. **Refused rather than replaced** with octet-stream: the comment two lines away says a media type is stated, never guessed, and substituting one for a value the index holds would be guessing on behalf of a damaged record. `null` is different — the schema says it means "not known", and octet-stream is the honest answer to that |
| D-105 | `primitives.ExternalLink` is the one external link: it checks the scheme, and carries `target`/`rel` once | accepted | Five sites rendered an API-supplied URL — `hit.source_url`, `source.url`, `artifact.url` — straight into `href`, with `target` and `rel` repeated at each and no scheme check, while the markdown path a few lines away had used `isSafeHref` since it was written. React 19 neutralises `javascript:` but passes `data:` and `vbscript:` through verbatim, and browsers block top-level `data:` navigation, so this was never more than latent — which is exactly why it survived. A refused href keeps the label as text with the URL beside it, the shape `Markdown` already used: the reader still sees what the record said and nothing is quietly dropped. Guarded structurally as well as behaviourally, because the defect *was* a shape |
| D-106 | The markdown inline-link pattern allows one level of balanced parentheses | accepted | `\([^)\s]+\)` stops at the first `)`, so a Wikipedia-style URL was cut at its inner paren and **both** the label and the href came out wrong — the link pointed somewhere real and different, which is worse than not linking at all. One level of balanced parens is what CommonMark allows without escaping and what real URLs use |
| D-107 | `errors.CODES` is built with `vocabulary.keysOf`, so a missing member is a compile error | accepted | `readonly ErrorCode[]` accepts a *subset*, so adding a seventh `ErrorCode` compiled silently while `failureFromBody` reclassified it as `internal` — discarding the server's own code and message on the one path whose job is to carry them. `vocabulary.ts` documents exactly this reasoning and has used the strict form since D-058; it is now the same helper, exported and imported, not a second copy. Verified by adding a seventh code to the declarations and watching `tsc` fail |
| D-108 | Every catalogue key is rendered by something, and `nav.skipToContent` renders a real skip link | accepted | 19 keys were referenced nowhere, and one was not clutter: `nav.skipToContent` was translated in both locales and `<main id="content">` was rendered, and nothing linked the two — so a keyboard user tabbed through the brand, the nav and the language switch on every page while the string that would have spared them sat in the catalogue. For accessibility a half-built affordance is the same as none, and the catalogue read as though the work were done. The link scrolls and focuses the target itself rather than relying on `href="#content"`, because D-060's `HashRouter` reads the fragment as a route. The other 18 keys are deleted and a test refuses the recurrence in both directions — a key nothing renders, and a key the code asks for that the catalogue lacks |
| D-109 | Dead code is removed rather than left to read as an API: `ids.is_source_id`, and the untracked `build/` shadow copy | accepted | `is_source_id` had zero references anywhere and looked like part of the id vocabulary. `build/lib/x2knwldg/` was a 41-file untracked copy of `src/`: nothing could resolve to it, but `grep`, `rg` and IDE search all hit it, and its staleness changed without notice — so every search over the tree returned two answers, one of them from a past version |
| D-110 | `prompts/04` states every field the bundle schema requires of a unit, and says a derived unit carries **no** `source` block | accepted | It stated the three obligations a derived unit owes beyond an ordinary one — `source_class`, `derived_from`, `derivation_note` — and omitted the four the schema *requires* of every unit: `id`, `kind`, `content`, `confidence`. Prompt 01 spells out a full template; prompt 04 did not, so a model reading only pass 4 emitted units `apply-bundle` rejects. The `kind` must come from `DERIVED_KINDS`, because a derived unit declaring a source kind is refused as `kind_source_class_mismatch` (D-022), and the absent `source` block is stated positively: adding one is how a synthesis comes to look like a quotation |
| D-111 | `requirements.txt` describes the extras as they are, and a CI job installs it | accepted | It described the `ui` layer as unfinished and said CI's test job installs `.[dev,legacy]`. `T-116` shipped `x2knwldg ui` and CI installs `.[dev,legacy,ui]`, so both statements were wrong — and **no job installed this file at all**, which is why nothing said so. A forward that nobody follows is a forward nobody has checked |
| D-112 | The `youtube` extra declares `requests` | accepted | `youtube.py` imports it directly: `_BoundedSession` subclasses `requests.Session` to force a default HTTP timeout, because `youtube_transcript_api` builds its own session and calls it with no timeout, which in `requests` means "block forever". It arrived only transitively through that dependency, and the guard around the import was commented "requests ships with the extra" — which no extra declared and nothing checked. If that dependency changes its HTTP layer the timeout silently disappears; declared, CI's extras job walks it |
| D-113 | The `legacy/upstream` boundary is enforced by tests, not stated in prose | accepted | The audit read the five unreferenced scripts as clutter to remove, and three of them are Whisper drivers that contradict a standing constraint (`CLAUDE.md`: never install or invoke Whisper or WhisperX). They are **kept**: `legacy/upstream/README.md` already says all of this and `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md` attributes them, so deleting attributed upstream history is not the fix. What was missing is enforcement — nothing checked that the package imports none of them, that no Whisper driver is reachable, or that the README's claim about which scripts still have tests is true. Three tests now do, which is this project's stated preference over an intention in a comment |
| D-114 | `ruff check` and `mypy` are configured in `pyproject.toml`, run by a CI `lint` job, and declared in the `dev` extra | accepted, amended by D-155 |  The project had **no** Python lint, format or type checking: no config, no CI step, no mention in the docs — while `web/` had `tsc --strict` with `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` and a job of its own. The audit called it the largest single asymmetry in the project, and the cost was not hypothetical: four imports left dead by a refactor earlier in this same session were found by running pyflakes by hand, and nothing else would have said so. The rule set (`E4`, `E7`, `E9`, `F`, `B`, `I`, `UP`) is a **floor chosen to be raised**; `E501` is out because the long explanatory comments in this tree are the documentation and reflowing them is a style decision, not a defect. **`ruff format` is deliberately not a gate**: adopting it would reformat 71 of 105 files in one commit and bury every real change, which is a decision to take on its own. mypy covers the package only — the tests build deliberately malformed documents to prove a validator refuses them, so annotations there would have to lie. Its 42 findings were fixed rather than silenced, and two were worth having: `io.is_finite_seconds` and `transcripts._is_finite_number` became `TypeGuard`s, which is both true and the only way the narrowing is legible; and the id constructors' `str` parameters became `Any`, because `validate_id_part` has always taken `Any` and refusing junk is the contract — a `str` annotation described a precondition no caller could satisfy |
| D-115 | The `tests` matrix runs on macOS as well as Linux | accepted | All five jobs were `ubuntu-latest`, and this project is developed and used on macOS. `routes/media.py`'s containment checks are the ADR-0003 boundary and they resolve paths; a case-insensitive filesystem answers `Path.resolve()` and `relative_to` differently from ext4, so the one boundary that most needs platform evidence had none. One macOS row rather than the whole grid: the platform matters, four interpreters on it do not |
| D-116 | A CI job serves the real API and runs the frontend suite against it | accepted | 13 frontend integration tests gate on `X2KNWLDG_API_BASE` and **no job set it**, so the tests that exist precisely because "a mock agrees with whatever the frontend assumed" never ran anywhere — the only tests in the tree that could not fail. `web/scripts/dev_api.py` was already a complete loopback server over the committed fixtures, so the job needed no new machinery. It also refuses a silent skip: a job that sets the variable but does not check the tests *ran* would pass on the same nothing. Run locally to prove it: all 13 pass against the real server |
| D-117 | Phase 2 starts on an **exactly pinned Sigma v4 beta**, with `T-202` as a compatibility gate and stable v3 as the one fallback before implementation proceeds ([ADR 0005](adr/0005-knowledge-map-client.md)) | accepted | Sigma v4's official site now calls the line beta and its quickstart documents `4.0.0-beta.5`; the maintainer reports the API full-featured and not expected to move, while still warning that combinations of newer features may expose bugs. Starting a new Map on v4 avoids knowingly building against the renderer API v4 replaces, and v4's declarative primitives fit the provenance/kind style matrix. A prerelease range would silently move under the lockfile; an exact pin plus the real 86/118 graph and the user's MacBook makes the risk observable before the phase accumulates code |
| D-118 | A Map is a **progressive, explicitly bounded snapshot**: the first `/api/graph` request is at most 500 nodes; pages accumulate by identity; an edge is drawable only after both endpoints have arrived; and `truncated` stays visible until the graph is whole ([ADR 0005](adr/0005-knowledge-map-client.md)) | accepted | D-059 permits an edge on one page to reach a node on another page, so rendering a page as if it were a graph either dangles the edge or invents the far node. Automatically walking an unbounded library before showing anything defeats overview-first loading. Accumulating the real pages, deduping the repeated straddling edge by `id`, and stating partiality preserves both performance and truth |
| D-119 | The Map's addressable state has one grammar, `mapLink`, and the selected entity is identified only by its existing three-part `global_id` ([ADR 0005](adr/0005-knowledge-map-client.md)) | accepted | `readerLink` already proved that a URL built in one place and read in another otherwise becomes two grammars. Selection, source scope, provenance and relation-vocabulary filters must survive reload; malformed values are ignored rather than coerced. Search hits without a `global_id` are not entities in v1 (D-023) and never receive an invented Map address |
| D-120 | No Knowledge Map operation is WebGL- or pointer-only: a semantic DOM companion exposes graph state, search, selection, neighbourhood, inspector and Reader navigation, while Sigma remains the visual presentation ([ADR 0005](adr/0005-knowledge-map-client.md)) | accepted | Sigma renders the graph through WebGL/Canvas layers, which do not themselves make thousands of graph objects keyboard-addressable or provide a useful text alternative. The project already requires keyboard accessibility and non-colour provenance signals. A small DOM control/result/inspector surface supplies the operable structure without putting heavy HTML on every rendered node or replacing the WebGL overview |
| D-121 | The Knowledge Map runs ForceAtlas2 **synchronously**, with no layout worker, until a measurement says otherwise ([ADR 0005](adr/0005-knowledge-map-client.md) § Gate result) | accepted | Measured, not assumed: 200 iterations over the real 86-node/118-edge graph take 2.7–9.0 ms on this MacBook — cold 8.5–9.0 ms, steady 2.7–3.4 ms — against a 16.7 ms frame. A worker would add a second lifecycle to kill, message passing for positions, and a failure mode where the layout outlives the renderer, in exchange for hiding a pass that does not block. The canvas plan permits a worker; `T-202` was told to adopt one only on evidence, and the evidence says no. The number is a *baseline*, not a threshold: revisit it against a measured graph an order of magnitude larger, and note that the first pass is the slow one, so a per-filter-change layout is the case to measure |
| D-122 | A Map node's drawn label is **not** the raw `label` field: `T-205` owns a truncation and density policy, and the full statement belongs to the inspector ([ADR 0005](adr/0005-knowledge-map-client.md) § Gate result) | accepted | The gate rendered the real labels and they are whole sentences — `library.py` sets a knowledge unit's label from `normalized_statement`, which is what the Reader wants and what a graph label cannot be. At 86 nodes the result is a pile of overlapping full sentences that hides the graph it is annotating, and the longest run past the container edge. Truncating for display is a presentation choice, not a rewrite of canonical text, so nothing is invented as long as the inspector still shows the statement in full. Sigma's own label-density settings are the mechanism; the decision here is that the Map must have a stated policy rather than inherit whatever a default draws |
| D-123 | A Map snapshot is **whole** only when the walk has finished, no edge is still pending, and either the API stated `truncated: false` or the loaded node count reached the stated `total`. `total: null` is unknown and blocks the claim rather than granting it | accepted | `truncated` is a statement about a *page*, and both repository implementations compute it against the whole filtered node set — so the **last** page of a multi-page walk reports `truncated: true` too. Treating it as sticky would mark a graph holding every node the filter matched as partial forever; treating a null cursor as completion is exactly what ADR 0005 invariant 4 forbids. Neither fact settles it alone, so the snapshot reports both and derives wholeness from the count. Measured on the real 86/118 graph: at 50 nodes per page the final page says `truncated` with all 86 nodes loaded, and at one node per page it takes 86 pages and up to 54 held edges to reach the same graph |
| D-124 | A projected node carries `x`, `y` and the API's `EntityRef` **verbatim**; a projected edge carries its `IndexedRelation`. No display attribute — label, size, colour — is stored on the graph; `T-205`'s style matrix belongs to the renderer's reducers | accepted | The Map is a view of canonical evidence, so a stored label would put a presentation decision — D-122's truncation among them — inside the data the inspector and the Reader read back, and a flattened record cannot be compared field by field, which is what D-125's refusal needs. It also means a restyle is a reducer change rather than a re-projection of the whole graph, and that `Object.keys` of a node's attributes is a test that no field was invented |
| D-125 | A repeated identity is the same record only if **every** field matches, where an absent field and a `null` one are the same statement. Any other difference is a `GraphConflictError` naming the field, and the page is refused **whole** | accepted | D-059 guarantees repeats — a straddling edge is returned on both of its pages — so a repeat cannot be an error by itself. The contract spells every optional field `field?: T | null`, so both spellings mean *not stated* and refusing over that would refuse a legitimate graph; but `confidence: 0.9` against `null` is two claims about one edge, and merging would draw a record no request returned. Refusing the page before inserting anything keeps the snapshot exactly as it was, so a refusal is not also a half-applied page to reason about |
| D-126 | The Map has **one** renderer lifecycle, owned by a framework-free `MapSession` reached through an injected factory. React decides only when to attach and when to kill | accepted | ADR 0005 invariant 10 is a *sequence* property — a replacement kills its predecessor, unmount kills the last, nothing outlives the route — and jsdom has no WebGL, so the sequence is only assertable against a fake. React 19's `StrictMode` double-invokes effects, so an attach that did not kill first would leak one WebGL context per mount on the very first page load, and a browser answers an excess by losing the *oldest* context — a blank canvas far from the cause. One class also keeps §8.6's "no second Sigma wrapper" structural rather than remembered |
| D-127 | Sigma is reached through a **dynamic** `import`, never a static one, and only from the Map route | accepted | Not a preference: `sigma`'s default primitives call `layerFill` while the module body evaluates, and it destructures `UNSIGNED_BYTE` off the global `WebGL2RenderingContext`, which jsdom does not define. A static import anywhere in the application's module graph is therefore a `ReferenceError` at load that fails the Library's and the Reader's suites for a module neither uses. Loading it where it is used also splits a 362 kB chunk (94 kB gzipped) off the two routes that draw no graph. A test fails if a static import reappears |
| D-128 | A continuation page re-settles the **whole** layout; placed nodes are not pinned | accepted | A page's new nodes arrive on their identity seeds (D-118), which say nothing about the structure the layout has already found — drawn as they are, a second page is a scatter of dots over a settled graph. `graphology-layout-forceatlas2` can pin a node, but only through a third node attribute, and D-124 lets the graph carry `x`, `y` and the record and nothing else. So the picture moves when more of the graph arrives, and that is the accepted cost of not storing layout state on the data |
| D-129 | A Map that cannot draw still reports the graph: the renderer's refusal is a stated state beside counts that remain true, and those counts precede the canvas in the DOM | accepted | `allowInvalidContainer: false` is kept because a graph drawn into a zero-sized box is a failure nobody can explain — which makes an unsized container, and a browser with no WebGL2, states the Map has to be able to render. The counts were also the only honest description available to a screen reader until `T-208` built the companion list beside them (D-142), so a Map whose account came *after* the picture would read as complete to anyone who never reached the picture |
| D-130 | The Knowledge Map is a **content browser**, with one progressive journey: Search → Preview/Peek → Focus → Quick Read. Preview and Quick Read stay inside `#/map`; the full Reader is reserved for reading the source in depth | accepted | The user's primary task is to find knowledge, judge what to open, read it and browse related knowledge without repeatedly leaving the graph. Bare circles provide topology but insufficient information scent, while routing every selection to the Reader creates the hub-and-spoke backtracking the Map is meant to prevent. The staged journey keeps overview and detail together without making every node a permanent card ([ADR 0005](adr/0005-knowledge-map-client.md)) |
| D-131 | Card text is **verbatim API content**, never a generated summary: preview and the on-stage selected card show returned text with visible truncation when needed; Quick Read prioritises the complete stored statement, recorded locator excerpt, active relation and derivation before technical metadata | accepted | `EntityRef.label` is already the pipeline's chosen `normalized_statement` or content and may be 4096 characters; its locator may carry the recorded excerpt and time. Re-summarising would create a third, unproven claim and obscure provenance. The UI may truncate a preview visibly, but Quick Read must expose the complete stored statement and never complete a missing field ([ADR 0005](adr/0005-knowledge-map-client.md)) |
| D-132 | Focus uses a **bounded card constellation** over the same graph: one primary selected Knowledge Card, at most one transient Peek card, active relation labels, and compact neighbour previews under a stated density policy. Every returned neighbour also appears in the semantic related list, so an unplaced on-stage card is never a hidden entity | accepted | Rendering HTML for every node defeats Sigma and creates an overlapping accessibility tree; rendering only dots recreates the blind-click problem. Sigma v4 explicitly supports graph-coordinate HTML/SVG overlays through `graphToViewport`, so a bounded overlay supplies readable focus while WebGL retains the overview. The semantic list is the completeness path when the viewport cannot fit every preview ([ADR 0005](adr/0005-knowledge-map-client.md)) |
| D-133 | A focus selection pushes `mapLink` history and browser Back restores the preceding focus **without unmounting the Map**; hover/keyboard Peek is transient and never writes history. Visual grouping and ordering use only stated relation, direction, vocabulary, provenance, source and identity — never inferred importance or a decorative quantitative axis | accepted | Exploration needs history to avoid disorientation, but URL history for hover would be unusable noise. The supplied radial/infographic references motivate a clear centre, labelled paths and restrained group regions, not a radar value the data does not carry. Deterministic grouping keeps the view reproducible and preserves the distinction between visual emphasis and evidence ([ADR 0005](adr/0005-knowledge-map-client.md)) |
| D-134 | A selection's **bounded neighbourhood is projected into its own transient structure and never merged into the drawn snapshot**. It is replaced whole when the selection or the depth changes, and the picture on the canvas stays `GraphSnapshot`'s | accepted | `GraphSnapshot` answers exactly one question — the pages the URL's three filters describe (invariant 5) — and its honest counts are read against the `total` the server counted for *that* question (D-123). A neighbourhood is a different question: it ignores `source_id` and `provenance_class` and reaches whatever is within `depth` hops. Merging it would draw nodes the filters exclude, push `nodes` past `knownNodeTotal`, and make `complete` a claim about a set nobody asked for. The projection is still `T-203`'s, refusal included, so a response contradicting itself is refused rather than merged (D-125) |
| D-135 | **`enableEdgeEvents` stays `false`.** An edge is never a pointer target; its relation, direction, vocabulary and provenance are named in the DOM instead | accepted | `T-204` left the setting off "until something selects an edge", and `T-207` is the task that would have. It does not: the Map's selection is `focus=<global_id>` and D-119's grammar can address nothing else, so a click on an edge has nowhere to go — the honest options were an unaddressable pointer target or a fifth URL parameter for a relation id. Edge *styling* needs no event either, since an edge's interaction state is derived from its own endpoints, so the active path lights up with picking still off and a hit-test pass per frame is not spent |
| D-136 | **Neighbourhood depth is a view control, not URL state.** `mapLink`'s grammar stays the four parameters D-119 froze | accepted | Depth bounds one request and changes nothing about which graph is drawn or which entity is selected, so it is the search query's case rather than a filter's (D-119 already excludes the query). A fifth parameter would also be a change to a frozen grammar made by a component, and `mapLink` is deliberately the only thing that spells a Map URL. The bound itself is still the contract's: 1–3, **ignored rather than clamped** above that, because the response echoes `depth` back and a clamped value would report a bound the reader never set |
| D-137 | The on-stage card overlay is **presentation only**: no button, link or field, `pointer-events: none`, `aria-hidden`, and a *sibling* of the container the renderer owns. Every action a card suggests lives in the DOM — the related list's controls, or a click on the mark itself | accepted | Three failures at once. A focusable card over a canvas builds a second accessibility tree over entities the related list already lists; a control that exists only inside the overlay makes WebGL the only way to reach it, which `T-208`'s gate forbids outright; and a subtree rendered *inside* the renderer's container is destroyed the first time `MapSession.kill()` empties it (which it must, because Sigma appends its own canvases there). Selection therefore reaches `focusEntity` from the mark or from a real control, and the overlay cannot introduce a second identity because it holds no handler at all |
| D-138 | Cards are re-placed when the camera **settles**, not on every frame, and none is drawn while it is moving | accepted | Anchoring is per-node, so following the camera exactly means re-placing every card on every frame — and the placement feeds the omission report, so that would re-render the related list and the search rail sixty times a second for one pan. The Map already answers this question for labels: `hideLabelsOnMove: true`, because text that reflows every frame is unreadable anyway. A card is the same argument one size larger, so it follows the same rule: two renders per gesture instead of sixty, and the cards return the moment the camera stops (`MAP_STAGE_SETTLE_MS`) |
| D-139 | The Map's honest states are **two total functions** over what the walk and the renderer already report (`mapState.ts`), and what they exist for is the four pairs they must not collapse: unasked ≠ empty, partial ≠ whole, refused ≠ empty, undrawn ≠ absent | accepted | Five states were already on this route and each was decided at its own render site, which is five independent conditions and no statement of the *distinctions*. The defect that shape produces is not a wrong message, it is two situations rendered as one true-sounding sentence — and one of them has happened here before: D-068 was an empty answer to a question nobody asked, presented as an answer. A snapshot with no page applied therefore has `counted: false` and prints no zeros; `complete` is read from `GraphSnapshot` and never recomputed (D-123); and the pages that arrived before a failure stay countable while the view says out loud that they are not an answer to the request that failed. A new state is a clause in one function, and the pairs are asserted directly rather than through the DOM |
| D-140 | A renderer that **never loaded** and a renderer that **refused this container** are two states with two messages | accepted | They have different answers. A dynamic `import` that fails is a browser with no WebGL2: nothing on that canvas will ever work, and the reader needs to know the picture is gone for good and everything else is not. A renderer that was reached and refused is almost always `allowInvalidContainer: false` on a stage that has not been laid out yet (D-129), which the next layout fixes. The single message said "A browser without WebGL2, or a container with no size" and sent readers of the second case looking for a different browser |
| D-141 | The canvas is not described as **drawing** until a live renderer holds the current graph, and `role="img"` with the label "Knowledge graph, drawn" is written only then | accepted | React runs a render and then its effects, so the render that first sees a page precedes the effect that hands the graph to the renderer — `T-207` found the same off-by-one-render in the constellation's first placement. Here it made the Map claim a picture for one render and then lose it again, which flipped the companion panel's step twice and (through the `<details>` defect in D-143) left it closed. It also matters on its own: an empty box announced as an image of the knowledge graph is a claim about content that is not there. The visible cost is that the zoom and reset controls arrive one render after the counts, which is honest — there is no camera until there is a renderer |
| D-142 | The Map has a **DOM companion** listing every entity it has drawn — the API's own order, the same card the search rail renders, bounded at 25 with the remainder counted, and deliberately **not windowed** | accepted | D-120 pairs the WebGL surface with a DOM one, and until now the DOM half could only be reached through a *query*: the rail lists what matches, the related list lists a selection's neighbourhood, and both need something typed or something selected first. So a reader with no pointer, no WebGL2 or a screen reader had the counts (D-129) and no way to reach the entities the counts were about — "essential content exists only on the canvas", which the phase gate forbids. Ordering by degree was rejected as the invented importance invariant 15 forbids, and because it would reorder the list under the reader on every accumulated page. Windowing was rejected because a row outside the DOM is a row no screen reader and no in-page search can reach, which costs exactly the claim the list exists to make (R20's argument, in the other direction) |
| D-143 | Panels are **one** `Disclosure`: `<details>`/`<summary>`, the panel's count *in the summary*, `preferOpen` following the journey's step and overrulable by the reader — and `open` written as a prop once at mount, imperatively after that | accepted | `T-207` left three panels competing for one screen and made Quick Read a `<details>`, which is where the sentence "collapsible rather than permanent competing panels" was left. Writing it once puts the *rule* in one place: a folded panel still states what it holds, because a disclosure that says only "Related knowledge" turns the screen into a row of doors. The imperative half is a defect found here: a `<details>` fires `toggle` **asynchronously**, including for a change a script made, so rendering `open={state}` turns every programmatic change into an event that arrives a task later and writes its value back — two preference changes in quick succession let the first event land after the second, and the panel closed itself with nothing clicked. Two consequences followed: the transient Peek moved out of the rail to the route, because a card rendered inside a collapsed `<details>` is a card nobody sees (still exactly one, invariant 13), and a guard fails if a second `<details>` appears anywhere in `web/src` |
| D-144 | Reduced motion is answered **twice, because there are two animators**: a blanket `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)` block for everything the browser animates, and `motion.ts` for the camera — which returns `undefined` rather than a duration of its own. Touch targets are 44 px on a coarse pointer and always for a `<summary>`, and a narrow screen shortens the stage rather than collapsing it | accepted | The stylesheet cannot reach a camera eased in JavaScript on a canvas, and the Map's zoom and reset are exactly that; a reader who has asked for less motion would have kept getting the glide. The blanket CSS rule is preferred over a list because the list is what rots — the next transition anyone adds is covered without their remembering the block exists. `undefined` rather than a number keeps the renderer's own easing and avoids a fifth Map constant chosen by argument and measured by nobody (`T-209`). A `<summary>` gets its minimum on every pointer type because it is a line of text, which is the hardest thing on the page to press. And the stage keeps a stated minimum on a narrow screen because `allowInvalidContainer: false` turns a collapsed container into a stated renderer failure (D-129) — a small window must not read as a broken Map |
| D-145 | The on-stage card policy tests **whether the card fits on the stage and whether it covers one already placed, over the card's measured footprint in four stated orientations**, rather than occupancy of a fixed grid cell; the reserved rectangle includes the mark the card points at; a neighbour that fits in no direction is counted under a fifth reason of its own, `no_room`; and the forced neighbour-label budget is **4**. The numbers are Chrome's own: a neighbour card is 320x296 and a primary one 416x176 at the shipped widths | accepted | `T-209` walked it. A 240 px square cell asks "is another card in this cell?" when the question is "would this card cover one?", and on the real fan-out it got both halves wrong at once: **7 of 8** neighbours were refused cards that would have fitted, and two cards were placed overlapping by two thirds of a card — the lower one covering the focused statement's identifier and the marker that says its text was cut, which is the silent cut D-131 forbids. Two anchors either side of a cell boundary can be a pixel apart; two in one cell can be 300 apart. The mark is inside the rectangle because two cards opening in opposite directions from marks four pixels apart overlap nothing and still point into the same four pixels. The four orientations exist because a card opens *towards* the middle so it will not be clipped, so two marks either side of the middle grow towards each other and meet — with only the preferred orientation, every focus in a twenty-entity sample placed its own card and not one neighbour's. And 12 forced labels drew nine sentences into a cluster 250 px across, because ForceAtlas2 pulls a node's neighbours towards it: a fan-out is the densest part of the picture and the worst place to bypass Sigma's own grid. The fit clause and the fifth reason came last, from *looking at the running UI* after the gate was green: two cards hung 21 and 69 px out of the top of the stage with the first line of their statements behind the search rail — the overlay is a sibling of the container, so nothing clips it — and the declared 248 px height turned out to be shorter than the 295 px a wrapped relation cue produces, so the rectangle being tested was smaller than the card being drawn. `no_room` is its own reason because "outside the visible stage" would send a reader to pan a camera that is not the problem |
| D-146 | **Selecting a focus brings it onto the stage**: `MapSession.frame` centres the focus *and its neighbours in the drawn graph* and sets the camera ratio from their extent, once per selection, with the reduced-motion argument every other gesture carries. `MAP_FOCUS_MARGIN = 1.2` is calibrated, not derived | accepted | Selection and the camera had never spoken. The camera framed the whole 86-node graph, so a focus sat wherever the layout had put it, its neighbourhood spanned about a tenth of the stage, and every neighbour card was refused for covering the focused one. Worse, Sigma's `zoomIn` zooms about the **centre of the stage** rather than about the selection, so a reader who selected a node and pressed it twice pushed the selection off the stage while the route went on saying, correctly, that the graph was drawn — a seam between two components that both knew where the focus was, which is D-069's shape again. The neighbours come from the *drawn* graph rather than from the bounded neighbourhood, because that answer arrives later and would move the camera a second time under a reader who had started reading. The margin was chosen by walking 23 focuses at five values and counting what the stage could carry: 1.2 is the tightest framing at which no neighbour's mark falls outside `MAP_STAGE_CARD_INSET`, and also the value that places the most cards, because zooming out further only pulls the marks back together |
| D-147 | A renderer that **refuses its container releases what it took**: `MapSession.attach` empties the container and loses the WebGL context explicitly before re-throwing. And the stage's CSS **minimum** height is load-bearing because `allowInvalidContainer: false` refuses only an *exactly* zero dimension | accepted | Both halves are `T-209` measurements, and the second inverts D-144's reasoning. Sigma takes its context in its constructor and validates the container afterwards, so a zero-sized stage throws with a live context attached and `MapSession` never receives the object whose `kill()` would release it: seven refused attaches left **seven contexts alive and seven canvases in the stage**, with no `webglcontextlost` for any of them, and leaving the route released none. Browsers cap live contexts at around sixteen and answer an excess by losing the *oldest*, so the symptom is a different Map going blank later — this is invariant 10, and no jsdom test could see it, because a fake factory throws without having created anything. The context is lost explicitly rather than left to the collector, which is what Sigma's own `kill()` does. On the second half: a stage two pixels high is *accepted*, drawn into and reported as a picture with `role="img"`, so the stylesheet's minimum is not a way of avoiding a stated refusal — it is what stands between a narrow window and an unreadable graph the route calls drawn |
| D-148 | The route reads **Escape on `window`**, and only while a Peek is open | accepted | `T-208` added "Escape dismisses the Peek from anywhere on the route" as a React `onKeyDown` on the route's own element, which only ever sees a key pressed while focus is *inside* it. A canvas takes no focus, so the one surface with no other way to dismiss a Peek — a pointer on a mark — was the one surface where the key did nothing, and jsdom could not have shown it because a test fires the event at the element it chooses. Bound only while a Peek is open, so the route adds no global key handler to a page with nothing to dismiss |
| D-149 | `interpolate` supports **one plural form**, `{count|singular|plural}`, chosen on `=== 1`; English uses it for the Map's count messages and Persian uses it nowhere | accepted | The browser walk read "1 hops from the focus", "1 related entities" and "1 returned relations name an endpoint the response did not return" off the real route — three plural errors in one sentence about one real neighbourhood, on a route whose whole argument is that the words on screen are the record's own. It is deliberately not a plural *library*: two alternatives on `=== 1` is English's rule, written out per message rather than inferred, and a locale whose rule differs simply does not use the form — Persian keeps the singular after a numeral, so its catalogue has no `|` in it and needs none. Placeholder parity between the catalogues is still asserted, over both forms |
| D-150 | Phase 2 remains functionally complete; a new Phase 2.1 visual-quality gate (`T-210`) blocks Phase 3 ([ADR 0006](adr/0006-map-visual-quality.md)) | accepted | The browser suite proves behaviour and accessibility, but the user rejected the produced composition against the approved visual references. Rewriting Phase 2's history would hide that distinction; proceeding to Canvas would preserve a known product-quality failure |
| D-151 | `T-211` must deliver user-approved high-fidelity Explore and Focus screenshots before production UI implementation begins ([ADR 0006](adr/0006-map-visual-quality.md)) | accepted | The current gap came from treating component correctness as visual design. A screenshot-level approval gate makes hierarchy, geometry and art direction testable before shared Map surfaces are refactored |
| D-152 | Explore and Focus use different presentations of the same graph state; Focus is a Directional Orbit with the selected card central, incoming relations left, outgoing right and actual hop as radius ([ADR 0006](adr/0006-map-visual-quality.md)) | accepted | One ForceAtlas composition cannot simultaneously optimise global topology and readable local content. Direction and hop are real fields; importance, clustering and decorative magnitude are not and remain forbidden |
| D-153 | The Map is a viewport workspace: status and controls float compactly, while Search, related knowledge and Quick Read use bounded drawers rather than document-flow sections that push the graph below the fold ([ADR 0006](adr/0006-map-visual-quality.md)) | accepted | At the reviewed desktop viewport the stage begins far down the page, breaking the core browse/read loop. Visual placement may change while DOM order, honest states and keyboard access remain intact |
| D-154 | The Map uses an editorial visual system and cannot pass without screenshot-based browser QA: no clipping, card overlap or labels under cards; active relation labels are horizontal; dark/light and English/Persian are verified ([ADR 0006](adr/0006-map-visual-quality.md)) | accepted | Colour tweaks cannot repair hierarchy or collision. The reference quality depends on restrained contrast, typography, space, readable paths and a deliberate centre, all of which require visual evidence in addition to behavioural tests |

| D-155 | The `lint` job installs the `mcp` extra, and `mcp_server.py` declares `MCPServer: Any \| None` with an aliased import rather than rebinding the imported name. Amends D-114 | accepted | D-114 put `mypy` in CI over `src/x2knwldg` and the job installed `.[dev,legacy,ui]` — no `mcp`. So `from mcp.server import MCPServer` was unresolved, `ignore_missing_imports = true` made it `Any`, and the file checked clean: **the one module that fails the check was the one module the check could not see**. With `mcp` installed it reports three errors (`Cannot assign to a type`, and two `Incompatible types in assignment`). `warn_unused_ignores = true` rules out a `type: ignore` — it would be unused when the extra is absent — so the fix has to be a *form* that passes under both dependency sets, and this one is verified clean both ways. The `extras` job did install `mcp`, but ran `pytest`, never `mypy` |
| D-156 | `vite.config.ts` joins the browser gate's type-check program | accepted | `ci.yml`, `browser/tsconfig.json` and a test docstring all claimed the gate would otherwise be "the only unchecked TypeScript in the repository". It was not: the `src` program includes only `src` plus the generated declarations, the browser program only `browser/` and `playwright.config.ts`, and neither `--listFiles` contained `vite.config.ts` — which owns the `/api` proxy that the `integration` and `browser` jobs both depend on. It goes in the gate's program because it is build tooling rather than shipped code, and needs the same `skipLibCheck`; the three claims are corrected rather than left to rot |
| D-157 | The zero-dependency job derives its creep list from `pyproject.toml` rather than restating it, and the fixture-reproducibility job uses `git status --porcelain` rather than `git diff` | accepted | The hand-maintained list named **7 of the 14** declared optional distributions, so `mcp`, `youtube-transcript-api` and `requests` could have crept into the core install with nothing to say so — and a list that has to be updated by hand whenever an extra grows a dependency is a check that decays silently. `pytest` is the one exemption, because that job installs it on purpose. Separately, `git diff` is blind to *untracked* files, so a generator that started writing an extra artifact regenerated "byte-identically" and passed; `git status --porcelain` sees the new file, which is the case the job exists to catch |

| D-158 | Run discovery is **one** function, `io.discover_run_dirs`, called by the scanner, `adapt_project` and `rebuild_library`; a directory resolving to one already discovered is reported as an **alias**, never walked twice | accepted | Three implementations of one rule disagreed three ways. `library._run_dirs` was a bare `glob` with neither the dot-directory guard nor the `library/` guard the other two had, so a rebuild indexed runs the scanner refuses — add `output/.staging/` and every canonical concept and `expresses_concept` edge disappears while `runs_skipped: 0` reports nothing wrong. And all three were missing the same third clause: `glob` **follows directory symlinks**, so `ln -s output/<id> output/latest` was discovered as a second run, produced every record twice, and `check_index_integrity` refused the **entire** index — counts all zero, every endpoint `503`, both real runs lost, and the message blaming a duplicate `video_id` that no directory declares twice. `_run_files` has excluded symlinks since D-100; discovery did not. The real directory always owns the location, whatever the sort order says, and the alias is *named* in `skipped_runs[]` rather than dropped, because a run that vanishes with nothing said is what D-043 exists to prevent |
| D-159 | The index opens `journal_mode = WAL` with a 5-second `busy_timeout` | accepted | The store executed exactly one pragma, `foreign_keys`. In the classic rollback journal a writer locks the whole database, so a reader during a build gets `database is locked` — which `_index_state` maps to `state='error'` with no counts, and `payload()` renders an absent count as `0`. The one endpoint that exists to be honest therefore reported `state: error`, *"the index cannot be read"* and `sources: 0, artifacts: 0` about an index that was intact and whose stored row said `building`. Two `x2knwldg serve` processes are enough to reach it. WAL lets a reader read the last committed generation while a build writes, which is both faster and truthful. **Residual, deliberately not fixed here:** a genuinely corrupt database still renders unknown counts as zeros, because the frozen `StatusPayload.counts` requires integers — widening it to `["integer", "null"]` is a breaking change and therefore a `schemas/v2/` decision (D-015), not a patch |
| D-160 | `SqliteRepository.open` catches `sqlite3.DatabaseError` as well as `FileNotFoundError`, and reports an unopenable file as `error` rather than `absent` | accepted | It caught only `FileNotFoundError`, so an index at mode 0, or a directory at that path, raised a bare `sqlite3.OperationalError` carrying no `code` and no `http_status`. `server/deps.build_repository` runs from `create_app`, so the **application failed to construct** — and a reader that cannot be constructed cannot say why, which is the reason the neighbouring branch already catches `DatabaseError` for the schema-version read. `error` and not `absent`: there is a file there, and why it cannot be read is a fact about it rather than about its absence |
| D-161 | `read_status` refuses a non-string status before testing membership | accepted | `status in RUN_STATUSES` hashes its left operand, so `{"status": ["PASS"]}` or `{"status": {}}` in a validator file raised `TypeError: unhashable type` out of a function whose whole contract is that a missing, unreadable or *unrecognised* file becomes `UNKNOWN` and never raises. `scanner` catches only `AdapterError` and `TypeError` is not in `cli.USER_FACING_ERRORS`, so one malformed file in one run killed `x2knwldg ui` on a raw traceback with no `{"status": "ERROR"}` envelope and took every other run in the project with it. Every other malformed shape was already an `AdapterError`; this was the one hole |
| D-162 | A scan writes `building` **carrying the previous `built_at`**, and a reader answers from a `building` index that names a generation. Extends D-086 to the crash path | accepted | `_write_state(connection, "building")` defaulted `built_at` to `None` and committed before `_apply`. Every write `_apply` makes is inside one transaction, so SIGKILL a refresh and the tables still hold the whole previous index — rollback worked — yet `state='building'`, `built_at=None`, `_require_ready` refuses, and every endpoint answers `503` until someone runs a **full rebuild** of a cache that was never damaged. D-086's own words apply verbatim: *"the stored records are exactly what they were and refusing every endpoint would cost the reader a library they can still be shown"* — it was reasoned about the caught-exception path and not the crash path, which is the likelier one. `building` with no `built_at` is still refused, because then no build has ever finished there; `status()` reports the state either way, so answering hides nothing |

| D-163 | `metadata.json` records `canonical_hashes` over `transcript.json` and `segments.json` at import, and `_evidence_integrity` checks the canonical documents three independent ways: the recorded digests, the preserved `raw/transcript.json`, and recomputing the segments from the captions | accepted | The evidence check had a hole the size of its own promise. `transcript_hash` was taken over `raw/source.<ext>` and compared back to *that same file*; the second comparison was against `transcript.json`'s **copy of the same string**. Nothing hashed the canonical documents — the files extraction reads and evidence excerpts are matched against.
