So writing a sentence the speaker never said into `transcript.json` and `segments.json`, leaving `raw/` untouched, produced a run where `apply-bundle` returned `PASS`, the evidence section returned `{"status": "PASS", "errors": []}`, and the invented quotation was printed into `report.md` while absent from `raw/source.srt`. `segments.json` is the one that matters most: `validate_provenance` matches every excerpt against a *segment's* text, not against the captions. The three checks are independent on purpose — the digests are exact and survive a change to the segmenter, the preserved copy holds for **every run ever imported** so no migration is needed, and recomputation is what catches an edit that also rewrites the digest. A run with no recorded digests is warned, not excused: it is still checked by the other two. `knowledge_units.json`, `relationships.json` and `coverage.json` are deliberately not sealed, because `apply-bundle` rewrites them |
| D-164 | Coverage `PASS` is verifiable: `window_size_sec` is required when the document claims `PASS` and bounds every window's width, `summary` is recomputed and compared, a named `source` unit's evidence must overlap the window naming it, and `apply-bundle` carries the scaffold's fields forward instead of dropping them. One shared `validate_coverage_links` | accepted | Coverage `PASS` was structurally unverifiable, and two bypasses were demonstrated on a real 30-minute run. **One:** collapse six 300-second windows into a single `[0, 1800]` window citing one unit at 0–10s → `PASS`. Nothing read `window_size_sec`, which `constants.py` calls part of the file format. **Two:** keep all six, mark every one `covered`, and name that same 0–10s unit in each → `PASS`, with 29 of 30 minutes unaudited by construction. Nothing tied a window's citations to its own span. `summary` and `covered_windows` were write-only — written by `create_pending_coverage`, read by nothing, so the two halves of one document could disagree. And `apply_extraction_bundle` **replaced** the scaffolded document, adding back only `schema_version` and `video_id`, so `window_size_sec`, `summary` and every window's `caption_ids` were silently dropped on the first apply: the fields the audit is checked against did not survive to be checked. The width bound is deliberately one-sided — subdividing a window is honest finer-grained work and the committed `partial-run` fixture does it; **coarsening** is the bypass. `summary` is recomputed on apply rather than carried, because it is derived from the windows the bundle just supplied. The unknown-reference and unaccounted-source rules existed twice, in `pipeline` and in `artifacts`, and the rule that was missing was missing from both; they are now one function |

| D-165 | `_cue_chunks` recognises a bare integer immediately before a timing line as the next cue's identifier, and a timestamp fraction may be any number of digits | accepted | Two intake defects in one grammar. The trim heuristic that removes the next cue's index from this cue's text required a **blank line** before it, and SRT written without blank separators is ordinary output from real muxers — so `body[-2]` was the previous cue's own text, the guard never fired, and every cue absorbed the next cue's number: `'One. 2'`, `'Two. 3'`. The corruption landed in the canonical `transcript.json`, which is the extraction input and, through the segments, the text every evidence excerpt is matched against. Separately the fraction was capped at three digits, so `00:00:01,0000` matched no timing grammar at all: every timing line fell through to body text and the file was rejected as *"No timestamped cues were found"* — contradicting the docstring's promise that a damaged timing line reaches `parse_timestamp` and is reported. The fraction is read as a decimal (`float("0." + digits)`), which is well defined at any length |
| D-166 | `_YOUTUBE_PATH_ID_RE` is anchored at the start of the path and bounded at the end, and covers `live`, `v` and `watch` as well as `shorts` and `embed` | accepted | It was used with `.search` and anchored at neither end, so `/shorts/dQw4w9WgXcQABC` — a malformed id an attacker chooses — matched its first 11 characters and filed the fetched captions under **a different real video's id**. That is the provenance poisoning `is_youtube_url` exists to prevent, one layer further in and from a host that really is YouTube. `/anything/shorts/<id>` matched too. Separately `/live/`, `/v/` and `/watch/<id>` — a premiere, the old embed spelling, and the path form of a watch page — all returned `None` while `is_youtube_url` returned `True`, which is the asymmetry that makes a real URL unusable |
| D-167 | A caption snippet carrying no `text` **field** is refused at the YouTube seam, and a transcript whose `character_count` is zero fails `transcript_integrity` | accepted | Both spellings defaulted to empty — `item.get("text")` to `None`, `getattr(item, "text", "")` to `""` — so a caption API that renames its text field produced cues that cleaned away to nothing, each marked `non_speech`, which is exactly what disarms the `empty_text` check. Six snippets carrying their text under `.content` gave `character_count: 0`, `validation.transcript: PASS` and exit `0`: full-length coverage windows over nothing anyone said. `character_count` was already computed and read by nothing, so the signal existed and nothing looked at it. A *cue* may be non-speech — the `[music]` concession is deliberate and stays. A whole transcript may not: there is no video whose entire caption track is `[music]`, and if there were it carries no knowledge to extract |
| D-168 | `fetch_metadata` carries the video's `duration`; `metadata.duration_sec` is the media's length when known and the caption span otherwise, with `captions_end_sec` beside it; the coverage windows are minted over it and completeness is checked against it | accepted | `duration` was in the yt-dlp info dict and was dropped, so `duration_sec` became the max caption `end_sec`, `create_pending_coverage` minted windows over that, and `timeline_not_fully_covered` compared coverage against it as well. A caption track covering the first ten minutes of a two-hour talk therefore yielded a **fully covered timeline** — and the check could not detect the truncation, because all three numbers derived from the same truncated one. Minted over the video, the same run produces twenty-four windows of which twenty-two have no captions to audit, which is the honest picture. The caption span is kept beside the duration rather than replaced: the gap between the two is the fact. `pipeline.run_duration_sec` is the one way to reach the number, so `apply-bundle` and the standing verdict cannot disagree about how long a run is |
| D-169 | All three keys `schemas/extraction_bundle.schema.json` requires are required by `apply_extraction_bundle`, no unknown top-level key is accepted, and `extraction_metadata` must be an object when present | accepted | Of the three required keys, `knowledge_units` was guarded (D-073) and `coverage` was guarded, while `relationships` was `bundle.get("relationships", [])`. A bundle that misspelled it or dropped it applied cleanly and wiped `relationships.json` to `[]`, reporting `PASS` over a run that had just lost every relationship it had. The schema's `additionalProperties: false` could not catch it: **nothing applies the schema at runtime** — `jsonschema` is a dev extra and appears nowhere in the package — so the required keys are required where the bundle is read or nowhere. A non-dict `extraction_metadata` was likewise discarded in silence while `extracted_at` was stamped anyway, so the run looked as though it had recorded provenance it had not |
| D-170 | `io.write_bytes` fsyncs the temporary file before the rename and the parent directory after it | accepted | `grep -rn fsync src` returned nothing, under eleven layered atomic replaces. The per-file claim — a reader sees the old file or the new one — holds against a concurrent *reader* and not against power loss: a rename can reach the disk while the data it names has not, and what a run then holds is a zero-length canonical file that `write_group`'s rollback can no longer undo, because the bytes it would restore are gone too. Every write in the package funnels through `write_bytes`, so both syncs happen in exactly one place. A directory that cannot be synced (Windows) never fails a write that has already landed |
| D-171 | `io.write_group` refuses two entries that name one file once case and Unicode composition are accounted for, on every platform | accepted | `ku-a` and `KU-A` are distinct to every validator — `validate_knowledge_units` dedupes case-sensitively and `ids.is_id_part` permits mixed case — and `artifacts._slug` does not case-fold. On macOS's default case-insensitive filesystem the second write won: `finalize` reported `PASS` and claimed four files, the disk held three, `report.md` listed both units, and one unit's note was simply gone. `write_group` built `written` as a set of *unresolved* paths, so no duplicate-path check fired, and no validator looks at the vault. The rule is case- and composition-insensitive **everywhere** rather than on the platforms where the filesystem is: `os.path.normcase` is a no-op on macOS, so a platform-accurate rule would leave this invisible to Linux CI, which is how it survived. Refused rather than repaired — which note should survive is not `write_group`'s to decide, and silently keeping one is the behaviour that lost the other |

| D-172 | The API parses the `Host` header itself and refuses a rebound name in the frozen error envelope, replacing `TrustedHostMiddleware`; every operation declares `400` and `500` | accepted | Starlette does `headers.get("host", "").split(":")[0]`, so `Host: [::1]:8931` became `'['`. The allowlist entries `"[::1]"` and `"::1"` were unreachable dead code and **every** request to an IPv6-bound server was `400` — including the UI root, at the exact URL `serve.py` prints and opens in the browser. Two of the three documented `--host` values produced a UI that answered nothing, and `localhost` was one of them, because `getaddrinfo(..., AI_PASSIVE)` returns `AF_INET6` first on macOS. Separately, `TrustedHostMiddleware` is *user* middleware and therefore sits outside the exception handlers, so its refusal was `400 text/plain "Invalid host header"` rather than `ErrorResponse` — the thing `errors.handle_http_exception` exists to prevent, in its own words: answering off-contract *"would teach a client that the envelope is optional"*. `test_api_hardening` asserted only the status code, so `assert_error`'s contract check never ran on this path. `/api/status` declared no `400` at all, and no operation declared the `500` its own handler produces |
| D-173 | `_parse_range` refuses a suffix range against a zero-length artifact, and a record path that cannot be resolved is a `404` | accepted | The `start >= size` guard was on the explicit-first branch only, so `bytes=-5` against a zero-byte artifact returned `(0, -1)` and the route answered `206 Content-Range: bytes 0--1/0` — a satisfied range over a representation with no bytes to satisfy it, where RFC 9110 requires `416 bytes */0`. The sibling branch, `bytes=0-`, already got it right. Separately a NUL in a record's `path` makes `Path.resolve()` raise `ValueError`, which is **not** an `OSError`, so `posixpath.realpath`'s own handler does not catch it and neither did the route: it reached `handle_unexpected` as an undeclared `500`. Same class as D-083/D-104 already closed in this file |
| D-174 | `server/errors.Unavailable` and `server/errors.RangeNotSatisfiable` are deleted, with the module docstring's claim about the first | accepted | Zero raises, zero imports, zero tests. `media.py` — the only route with that case — defines its own `MediaUnavailable` and its own `RangeNotSatisfiable` carrying a `size` attribute the copy here lacked, and imports only `ApiError, NotFound`. The docstring actively advertised one of them as something *"routes do raise themselves"*. A module docstring describing a class no caller can reach is a claim about the code that the code does not make |
| D-175 | A graph response carries at most `MAX_GRAPH_EDGES` edges, `truncated` says when it was cut, and both frozen payloads declare `maxItems` | accepted | `limit` bounded the *nodes* and nothing bounded the edges, and the two are not the same size — an edge list is quadratic in the nodes it connects. On a 600-node all-pairs index, `limit=500` returned **349,500 edges in an 83 MB response at 265 MB peak allocation**, built entirely in memory from one unauthenticated `GET`, with `page.total: 600` giving the client no signal. `GraphPayload.edges` declared no `maxItems`, so nothing in the contract said otherwise. The neighbourhood payload has the same shape and the same absence, so one helper bounds both — a bound that exists in one of two views is not a bound. The Map cannot draw that many edges either, so this is a limit the reader was already living inside |
| D-176 | A renderer that refused its container is re-attempted when the stage reports a size it has not already refused | accepted | The draw effect depends on `[graph, snapshotId, pages, factory]` — `stageBox` is not among them — and the resize effect only calls `session.resize()`, which is `this.renderer?.resize()` and therefore a no-op once the refusal has killed the renderer. So `data-map-canvas="refused"` was permanent until the reader happened to change a filter, on the commonest cause of a refusal: a stage that has not been laid out yet. Both `mapState.ts` and the shipped string in `i18n/catalog.ts` promise *"the next layout recovers it"*, and there was no path by which it could. Keyed on the box that was tried rather than on the fault object, so a container that is genuinely unusable is refused once rather than retried in a loop |
| D-177 | A snapshot with no page yet retires the picture the previous snapshot drew | accepted | `graphWalk.open()` bumps `snapshotId` and resets `pages` to 0, the draw effect returned early, and nothing detached — so the live renderer kept snapshot N−1 on screen while the counts panel unmounted and the stage took `aria-hidden="true"`. The result was a visible, interactive-looking picture of question A, hidden from assistive technology, beside a route describing question B. The browser gate missed it because `renderer.spec.ts` waits for the stage to reappear *after* the page lands |
| D-178 | The camera's framing effect drops `pages` from its dependencies | accepted | With `pages` in there, every merged continuation page re-ran `session.frame(...)`: select a node, zoom in to read it, press `Load more`, and the camera animates back, discarding the reader's pan and zoom. The comment directly above the effect says it *"fires once per selection"* — with `pages` it fired once per selection *per page* — and D-128 in the same file is precisely about not moving the picture under the reader |
| D-179 | `RouteErrorBoundary` wraps the routes, inside `Shell` | accepted | There was no error boundary anywhere in `web/src`: no `ErrorBoundary`, no `componentDidCatch`, no `getDerivedStateFromError`, and no `onCaughtError`/`onUncaughtError` on the root. React's behaviour without one is to unmount the whole tree, so any uncaught render error gave a blank document with no retry and no statement of what happened. ADR 0005 names *"an error throw"* as a path invariant 10 must survive, and a root that unmounts leaves nothing to observe that `MapSession`'s cleanup ran — the one thing between a throw and a leaked WebGL context. Inside `Shell` so the navigation, the language switch and the skip link survive: a reader who hits one is a click from somewhere that works. Reset is by key, so a retried view is a fresh subtree rather than the failed one asked to resume with whatever state it died holding |
| D-180 | A control that removes itself on activation moves focus to its enclosing region, through `withFocusRescue` | accepted | Seven of them dropped focus to `<body>`: both `Load more` buttons on the Map, the Library's, the Reader's two, `Stop loading`, and both `Clear search` buttons. Tab to `Load more`, press it, the final page arrives, the button unmounts with no focus target named — `document.activeElement === document.body`, the keyboard journey ends mid-page, and the next `Tab` restarts at the top of the document. `MapView.access.test.tsx` covered none of it, because a walk that clicks with a pointer never notices where focus went. The check runs after the commit and only moves focus if it was actually lost, so a `Load more` that survives keeps it — moving focus off a button the reader means to press again would be its own defect |
| D-181 | The Peek closes on blur **unless** focus is moving into the Peek card | accepted | `handlers(globalId).onBlur` called `close(globalId)` unconditionally, so the very Tab that would move focus *toward* the card unmounted it first: the card's own "Close the peek" button existed in the DOM only while a different element held focus, which made it unreachable from the keyboard. Four consumers were affected. The pointer path was fine and Escape worked, which is why nothing else showed it |
| D-182 | The browser gate states what it cannot prove: `<StrictMode>`'s double-invoke is asserted against the jsdom fake only | accepted | `playwright.config.ts` serves `npm run build` through `vite preview`, which is production React, where `<StrictMode>` does not double-invoke effects — which is *why* `{ created: 1, live: 1 }` holds there on first load without `MapSession` doing anything. But `mapSession.ts` justifies its whole design by that double-invoke leak, and the gate cannot exercise it. Recorded rather than papered over: what the gate proves, and can prove nowhere else, is that repeated real attaches release real WebGL contexts; the two halves together are invariant 10, and neither harness can state both |
| D-183 | The gate's `GL_INVALID_OPERATION` allowlist records what it costs, and the condition for removing it | accepted | `sigma` is pinned exactly at `4.0.0-beta.5` — right for a prerelease — and the consequence is that the gate must allowlist the beta's own GL noise, so it cannot distinguish that class of *real* GL error from it. No upstream fix arrives without a manual bump, and `npm view sigma dist-tags` still offers no stable 4.x (`latest` is 3.x, which this Map is not written against). The bump condition is written where the allowlist is: take the first stable 4.x, delete the allowlist, run the gate — green means the blindness is gone, red means those errors were always there |

| D-184 | `constants.ERROR_CODES` is the one D-030 taxonomy; `server/envelope.py` re-exports it and `mcp_server.py` reads it, and a malformed video id is `invalid_id` from every MCP tool | accepted | Two lists, both described as "D-030's taxonomy", already disagreeing: the MCP server said `internal_error` where the envelope and the frozen `ErrorCode` enum both say `internal`. **No test imported both**, so nothing could see it, and an agent reading an MCP reply got a code outside the closed vocabulary the HTTP contract publishes — with the MCP server's own test asserting the divergent value. It lives in `constants` for the reason `MAX_PAGE_LIMIT` does: two statements of one fact was the defect. Related and fixed with it: the same malformed id was `invalid_id` from `get_extraction_segment` and `invalid_request` from `search_video_knowledge`, contradicting the rule `_run_dir` states in as many words — a rejected id is `invalid_id`, and collapsing it hides a traversal attempt behind an ordinary refusal |
| D-185 | "Seconds" is one rule in four tiers in `io`: `is_finite_seconds`, `is_non_negative_seconds`, `require_seconds` (in the caller's exception type) and `seconds_or_none`. Every guard in the package delegates | accepted | `io.is_finite_seconds`'s docstring claimed to be "the one predicate behind every timestamp guard in the package" and named four deferrers. **Two of them deferred.** `ids._require_seconds` and `segmenter._require_seconds` were near-verbatim reimplementations that never imported `io`, identical to each other but for the exception type. And a fifth guard the docstring did not name — `query._seconds` — had **no finiteness check at all**, so `inf` and `NaN` came back as floats and became sort keys on which every comparison is `False`: a search result ordered by a value that cannot order, which is the exact failure the docstring says is excluded. Six implementations of one rule, described as one. The three messages the coercers raise are kept word for word, because they were already identical |
| D-186 | `dev_api.py` accepts `--fixtures`, mutually exclusive with `--project-root` | accepted | The flag was named in the script's own module docstring, in `web/src/map/README.md` as a copy-pasteable command, and in a CI comment — and argparse had never heard of it, so every one of those exited `2`. Adding the flag rather than deleting the mentions: it is the explicit spelling of the default, and "serve the fixtures *and* this project" is now refused rather than silently resolved one way |
| D-187 | Four rotted counted claims corrected, and `tests/test_docs_citations.py` grows guards for the mechanical ones | accepted | That file enforces that a document cites a *symbol* rather than a line, on the argument that "a symbol name fails loudly — `grep` returns nothing". **Nothing in the repository ran that grep**, so every semantic claim was unguarded, and four had rotted: §7.2 said CI is "five jobs" and installs `.[dev,legacy]` (nine, and `.[dev,legacy,ui]`); `index/README.md` said "the two FTS5 tables" (there is one — the second `USING fts5` is a probe `connect` drops); `web/README.md` and §3 both said "every frozen endpoint now has a caller" (`getArtifact` has none, and the reason is worth stating: nothing in the UI reads an artifact's *record*); and `MAP_LABEL_NEIGHBOUR_BUDGET` was quoted as 12 in a README **and in a source comment** after D-145 lowered it to 4 a hundred and thirty lines away in its own file. The new guards count the CI jobs, the FTS5 tables, the label budget and the endpoint-caller census from the sources they are about — the four that can be counted mechanically. The rest stay unguarded and known to be |
| D-188 | `SqliteRepository` memoises the answers that are the same for every page of one walk, keyed by the index generation | accepted | Two paths were paying a whole-collection cost on **every page** of a walk that asks the same question each time. `_total` restarted from row zero and JSON-parsed every row: on 4000 entities filtered by `source_id` — which `/api/sources/{id}/entities` always supplies, so this is the common path and not an edge — one page cost 17 selects and 4051 parsed rows, and an 80-page walk cost 324,158 rows and 2.08 s. And `search` re-ran the whole FTS retrieval and re-ranked the entire hit list per page before slicing it: 0.062 s per page, 3.79 s to walk 61 pages, ranking 4003 documents 61 times — reintroducing per page the cost ADR 0004's D-042 removed from the oracle, whose own words are *"Search costs one pass over the library per repository, not one per page."* Correctness comes from the generation key `(state, built_at)` rather than from hoping nothing changes: the state row is read on every call anyway, so a rebuild landing mid-walk empties the memo. Bounded at eight entries, because a cache with no bound is a second index. Measured after: the filtered 80-page walk is **0.04 s** |
| D-189 | `WORKFLOW.md` §5 lists all four accepted bundle keys, with which are required | accepted | It said the bundle has *"exactly three required keys, and the schema sets `additionalProperties: false`, so no other top-level key is accepted"*, and listed three. The schema declares **four** properties: `extraction_metadata` is accepted, `artifacts.py` consumes it into `metadata["extraction"]`, and all three committed fixtures carry one. An agent following the §5 table silently dropped the provenance record. This is the one drift finding that changes what a model produces, which is why it went first |

| D-190 | `main` carries a repository ruleset: pull request required, force-push and deletion blocked, and all **17** CI check contexts required and up to date, with no bypass actors | accepted | `gh api repos/Ssisakhti/X2KNWLDG/branches/main/protection` returned `404 Branch not protected` and `.../rulesets` returned `[]`. All nine jobs — including the browser gate that exists because jsdom cannot witness WebGL, and the zero-dependency job that ADR 0001 invariant 5 rests on — were **advisory**: a red run blocked nothing, and every fix recorded in this file could be reverted by the next push with CI's objection sitting unread in a tab. Every other gate finding was downstream of this one, which is why it went first. The 17 contexts are the nine jobs with both matrices expanded, so a job that stops running is a missing check rather than a silent pass. No bypass actors, deliberately: a gate the owner can step around is a gate whose state nobody can read off the repository. Direct commits to `main` end here — `a257d3d` was the last |

### ⚠️ D-011 is **additive** — do not "clean up" the 2-part ID

`.claude/commands/kg_navigator.md` **mandates** the 2-part form `<video-id>:<knowledge-unit-id>` for `output/library/graph.json` nodes, and `library.rebuild_library`, through `ids.make_library_id`, emits exactly that.

**Rewriting `library.py` to emit 3-part IDs breaks that skill.**

Required approach:

- `library.py` keeps `id` in its current **2-part** form.
- It **gains** an additive `source_type` field and a 3-part `global_id`.
- The index, API, and board files use the **3-part** form as identity.
- No canonical file loses a field.

A future session must not consolidate these into one form without also updating `kg_navigator.md`.

| D-191 | `T-211`'s Explore and Focus compositions are approved; Phase 2.1 implementation proceeds in the fixed order `T-212` → `T-213` → `T-214` → `T-215`, and the committed sources in `docs/mockups/T-211/` are the reference `T-215` regenerates and compares the running UI against | accepted | ADR 0006 clause 2 requires explicit approval of both screenshots before any production Map surface is refactored, and the approval arrived on 2026-09-03. Three things are settled by it and are no longer open for re-litigation during implementation: the Map is a viewport workspace whose document does not scroll; Focus is a Directional Orbit whose radius is `hops` and whose sides are relation direction; and below the orbit's minimum width the answer is **fewer cards with counted omissions**, never smaller text — at 390 px there is no orbit at all. The mockups also fix a rule the implementation inherits: stage placement is computed in physical pixels and must use physical `left`/`top`, because a logical inset mirrors an already-mirrored coordinate (D-012's stated exception, and the cause of the RTL drawer defect) |
| D-192 | `T-212` composes the Map as a viewport workspace: `Shell` gives `/map` a `var(--bar-height) 1fr` frame, the stage is the field rather than a band on a page, every control is a bounded floating surface, and `placeConstellation` refuses a card that would be drawn under one ([ADR 0006](adr/0006-map-visual-quality.md)) | accepted | D-153 asked for the graph to occupy the usable route viewport, and the measurement is what makes this a decision rather than a restyle: at 2852×1688 the focused document was 5795 px and is now 1688 px, the viewport itself, and the stage moved from 790 px down a document to 56 px down a workspace. Four things are settled by it. **The route names the composition, not the view**: `Shell` holds the workspace path, because how tall the bar is and whether the document scrolls are facts about the frame, and a child that reported them upwards would have to do it in an effect — one render of the Map in the document composition first, which on this route means a renderer created against the wrong box. **The chrome is measured, never stated as insets**: the composition mirrors under `dir="rtl"`, and an inset per edge is the defect D-191 carries forward — so the surfaces marked `data-map-chrome` are read back with `getBoundingClientRect` in the stage's own coordinates and handed to the policy as rectangles, which is the same overlap test the crowding clause already ran. A card refused for one is `no_room`: the mark *is* on the stage, so `off_stage` would send a reader panning a camera that is not the problem. **The drawer's width comes out of the field only at SPEC §5's `full` tier**, because subtracting 560 px from a 1280 px viewport leaves a field too narrow to place the 416 px primary card beside its own centred mark — 452 px needed on one side, 344 available on either — and the card D-132 guarantees then hangs over the edge; the browser gate measured exactly that, at 5 px. **Below 48rem the route keeps its document composition**, which is a scope boundary rather than a compromise: SPEC §5's third tier is the orbit's narrowest and belongs to `T-213`. The four departures from the approved `SPEC.md` are recorded in its §13, the largest being that the camera's controls share the inline-end rail with the drawer instead of being painted over by it — a *Focus Not Obscured* failure in the approved capture itself |
| D-193 | `T-213` makes Focus a Directional Orbit: `placeOrbit` replaces `placeConstellation` in the one module that answers "where may a card go", the composition reads no camera, direction is taken from the **focus's** end of each relation, and a mark further out is drawn from the card it is actually joined to ([ADR 0006](adr/0006-map-visual-quality.md)) | accepted | ADR 0006 clause 3 asked for it and the measurement is what makes it a decision rather than a restyle: at 2852×1688 the composition places 7 of 8 neighbours and counts 1, against 4 and 4, with zero clipped cards, zero card/card overlaps, zero cards or pills under a floating control and zero pills without a clear seat; at 1440×900 it places 2 and counts 6, against 1 and 8. Five things are settled by it. **The camera is no longer an input to placement**, and that is the change everything else follows from: the old policy asked `MapSession.nodePosition` where each mark was, so the picture moved with a pan, had to be erased mid-gesture and replaced on a trailing timer, and two of its five refusal reasons (`not_loaded`, `off_stage`) were facts about a camera and an accumulated graph. All of it is gone — the per-frame subscription, the settle delay, both reasons — and a neighbour whose page the walk has not reached now still has a direction and a hop count, so it still has a card. **Direction is read from the focus's end.** `ActiveRelation.direction` is stated from the entity the relation is listed under, so `exemplifies` running `KU-000029 → KU-000028` is `outgoing` in that neighbour's own row and `incoming` to the focus; reading the field as-is put every card on the wrong half of the field, and only the real graph showed it. `fromNearEnd` inverts once, and the side and the pill read the same answer. **A hop-2 edge leaves a real parent.** `projectNeighbourhood` records `parentId` and `toParent`, chosen deterministically as the smallest `global_id` one hop closer, because a breadth-first frontier hands back whichever predecessor the adjacency offered first and two runs would draw two pictures; a card whose parent has none is withdrawn and counted as `unanchored` rather than drawn from the centre, which would state a relation the records do not contain. **A reserved box has to be an upper bound over the drawn card**, and `web/scripts/measure_orbit.ts` is how that is kept: at 320 px wide Chrome laid the shipped card out at 186 px against `SPEC.md` §6's 148, so the reservation was smaller than the card and a pill was seated in space the policy believed was empty. **SPEC §4's band clamp is a search, not an inset**: the search rail at the `full` tier is 424 px tall against the composition's 150 px inset, and the only adjustment a card has — pushing outward along its arm — walks it *towards* the field's inline edge, which on the incoming side is where that rail is. Narrowing the band moves the whole side inwards instead. The five departures from the approved `SPEC.md` are recorded in its §14, the largest being that the `compact` tier's card boxes are smaller than the mockup drew at 1440, because they have to fit the 900 px floor §5 gives that tier |
| D-194 | `T-214` puts the editorial visual system on the composition, and closes the last clause of ADR 0006 clause 5 that was still open: a node the Directional Orbit has carded loses its canvas label, and an edge whose both endpoints are carded loses its relation label ([ADR 0006](adr/0006-map-visual-quality.md)) | accepted | The clause is "graph labels may not render under cards", and until now they did: the focused node's label and up to four neighbour labels were drawn on the canvas underneath the very cards carrying the same statements, in less text and without the cut marked. Four things are settled by it. **The rule is a set of ids, not a flag.** `MapViewState.cardedNodes` is what the orbit placed, so a neighbour it *counted* keeps its label and is still named — hiding every label in Focus would have taken a name away from the neighbours that have no card, which is the opposite of what the clause is for. The card clause is checked before the interaction, because the focused node is always carded and `selected` would otherwise force its label straight back under its own card. **Kind hue is a cue and never a surface.** `KindBadge` renders an 8 px `KIND_FAMILY_COLOUR` swatch beside the record's own kind token — the same table the legend explains and the mark is drawn with — and a stylesheet rule asserts no card is filled with it, because a card tinted by kind reads as a category with a status and the only classification this project colours a surface with is provenance. **The accent is not the rail.** The focused card is the centre by size, a doubled border, an accent ring with a glow, and a light-mode-only ground tint; its inline-start rail stays provenance's, because confusing "the one you asked for" with "where this came from" is worst on the one card being read. **The clauses are stylesheet rules rather than review notes**: `visualSystem.test.ts` checks them mechanically, including that nothing keys a size, an opacity or a colour to a `confidence` — ADR 0006's rejected fourth alternative, asserted rather than remembered. The two departures from the approved `SPEC.md` are in its §15, the larger being that `--edge-faint` is not implemented at all: Explore's edges are WebGL, a canvas cannot read a custom property, and `MAP_DIMMED_EDGE_OPACITY` is the production equivalent in the one table a renderer can reach |
| D-195 | `T-215`'s gate is built, green and **not sufficient**: the geometry, accounting, direction and workspace clauses are asserted per scenario in a real browser, and the acceptance of the pictures remains the user's, so the task is recorded `PARTIAL` rather than done | accepted | R21's whole argument, applied to the task that closes it. The measuring moved out of `scripts/measure_orbit.ts` into `browser/composition.ts` so the script that produced §14's numbers and the gate that holds them are one implementation; sixteen scenarios then assert the clauses ADR 0006 lists, per scenario, over the seams `T-212`–`T-214` published — and the recorded numbers hold unchanged, including in Persian and in light, because the placement reserves the tier's boxes rather than the text's. Three things this decision fixes for anything that follows. **The gate walks the mockups' own entity when the served library has it**: two pictures of two different neighbourhoods cannot answer whether the build reproduces *these* compositions, and over the committed fixtures it falls back to the busiest entity, holds every clause, and asserts none of the recorded numbers. **One clause is asserted outside the browser and says so**: a canvas label has no DOM node and Sigma exposes no instance, so `labelPolicy` and its unit tests hold "no graph label under a card" and the browser holds the half that has a rectangle — a relation pill over a card. Claiming all four clauses were in the browser would have been the more comfortable sentence and a false one. **A green gate is where the review starts.** The comparison found three composition differences from the approved set (`SPEC.md` §16); the largest is that a node's size is stated in graph units and Sigma scales it with the camera, so Explore's quiet field is a function of viewport width and is not quiet at 2852 px. None is a geometry violation, which is exactly why a person still has to look |
| D-196 | The `T-215` captures are **not accepted as they stand**; the three composition differences its comparison found become `T-216`, a fifth child of `T-210`, and `T-215`'s acceptance is taken up again against `T-216`'s captures | accepted | The decision the gate exists to make possible, made the way ADR 0006 clause 5 says it must be — by a person looking at two pictures, not by a suite reporting green. What `T-216` inherits is six clauses and one open question. The clauses: camera-independent mark and edge size, an overview label ration *if* R1 leaves one needed, the `compact` tier's collapsed search and counts chips, a recorded judgement on the drawer Explore mounts with nothing focused, edge weight, and the re-run. The open question is R1's own: Sigma's `itemSizesReference: "positions"` is why a wider field draws bigger marks, and moving to `"screen"` fixes that by freezing size under zoom — which silently retires D-122's zoom rule, because `labelRenderedSizeThreshold: 14` is a threshold nothing would ever cross again. So the sizing model and the zoom rule are one decision and `T-216` records it as one. Two things are **not** open: the orbit's numbers are a regression surface — 7 / 1 at 2852×1688, 2 / 6 at 1440×900, 1 / 7 at 1280×720, every geometry clause zero — and the gate that measures them is written, so the remediation is judged by the same instrument that found the differences rather than by a new one written to agree with it |
| D-197 | A Map mark's size is **screen pixels scaled by the field's width**, and the camera is the only other thing that may change it: `itemSizesReference: "screen"` plus `markFieldScale`/`edgeFieldScale` in the one style table, with `minEdgeThickness` lowered below the thinnest thickness the table declares. D-122's zoom rule is **re-expressed, not retired** ([ADR 0006](adr/0006-map-visual-quality.md)) | accepted | The decision D-196 left open, and neither half of it was what D-196 assumed. Sigma's `scaleSize` divides by `zoomToSizeRatioFunction(ratio)` and *then*, only under `"positions"`, multiplies by the framing — so **clamping the ratio function could never have fixed this**, because that function is handed the camera's ratio and the term that made a wider window draw bigger marks is the field's pixels per graph unit, which sits outside it. And `"screen"` does **not** freeze size under zoom: the division happens in both modes, so a mark still grows as `1/sqrt(ratio)` coming in. The two modes differ by exactly one factor and by nothing else. So `labelRenderedSizeThreshold` survives as a camera rule and is re-derived rather than deleted — it is **6**, because it must sit below the smallest mark any field draws at rest (6.1 px) or it would silence a *circle* while a diamond in the same cell spoke, and `NODE_PROVENANCE_MARK`'s four radii exist to make four shapes read as one weight, not as a ranking (ADR 0005 invariant 15). The ration moves to Sigma's grid at `labelGridCellSize: 560`, measured at ten labels over 86 marks at 2852×1688 and five at 1440×900 against the reference's eight and five. The viewport scale is not invented here either: `SPEC.md` §3 specifies it in words and this is the first build in which that sentence is true |
| D-198 | Explore's edges are drawn at `MAP_QUIET_EDGE_OPACITY`, a third opacity level between the dimmed and the active, which closes `T-214`'s one unimplemented clause ([ADR 0006](adr/0006-map-visual-quality.md)) | accepted | §6 proposed `--edge-faint` for "the quiet Explore field" and D-194 recorded it as unimplementable: Explore's edges are WebGL and a canvas cannot read a custom property. The number belongs in the one table a renderer can reach, and it is a *third* level rather than a reuse of `MAP_DIMMED_EDGE_OPACITY`, because "nothing is focused" and "something else is focused" are different sentences: 0.32 for the overview's own web, 0.25 for structure a focus has pushed back, 1 for the active path. A provenance hue at that opacity still says which of the three provenances a relation has, so the quiet costs nothing ADR 0005 invariant 9 requires — and the **marks are deliberately not quieted with the edges**, because ADR 0006 clause 3 has marks and structure dominating with text on demand, and quieting both produces a grey picture rather than a quiet one |
| D-199 | At the `compact` tier the Map's floating surfaces are chips: the search rail is closed to its trigger, the graph filters fold to theirs, and the account folds to its two headline numbers ([ADR 0006](adr/0006-map-visual-quality.md)) | accepted | The three surfaces `T-215`'s comparison named, each fixed as the finding stated it rather than redesigned. The rail used to decide `preferOpen` for itself as `focus === null`; the route decides now, because SPEC §5 gives that tier a search "closed to its trigger" and only the route measures the field — and an *unmeasured* field counts as wide, because `orbitTier(0)` is `stack` and a rail that mounted closed on every first paint would put D-130's own opening step behind a click at every viewport. The filters and the account become disclosures rather than bounded scrollers, because the rule this codebase already has for a panel that competes for a screen is that a folded panel states what it holds: the filter chip says how many filters are applied and the account chip says *86 nodes · 118 edges*. **D-129 is intact and is checkable rather than promised**: the account still precedes the stage, the counts a test or a screen reader reads are attributes on the section a folded disclosure never hides, and it opens itself whenever the picture is not being drawn — which is the case D-129 exists for. It moved no card: the counts float went from 261 px to 139 px at 1440×900 and the recorded 2 / 6 is unchanged |
| D-200 | **Explore mounts no drawer.** Quick Read and the related list appear when something is focused and not before, which reverses `T-212`'s choice ([ADR 0006](adr/0006-map-visual-quality.md)) | accepted | The judgement D-196 asked to be recorded either way. `T-212` kept both panels mounted on the argument that a panel which disappears cannot say that nothing is selected, and three things outweigh it: ADR 0006 clause 4 allows one primary drawer to open *on demand*; SPEC §2 gives Explore four surfaces and none of them is a drawer, and both approved Explore captures leave that corner empty; and the sentence was never this drawer's only home — `MapSearchRail`'s focus row says "Nothing is focused. Choose a result to focus it." on the one surface SPEC §2 *does* give Explore, which is also the step D-130's journey is on while nothing is selected. What settled it is the measurement D-201 added: two collapsed panels saying "nothing focused" are 4.5 % of an 844 px field, and the whole approved `compact` composition spends 10.3 % on its chrome. The tests that held the old behaviour now hold the new one and assert what was not lost, rather than being relaxed |
| D-201 | The browser gate asserts a **per-tier bound on the share of the field floating chrome may cover**, measured by one implementation over both capture sets — and at the `compact` tier that bound is a ratchet rather than the reference's own share, because the share and the recorded card counts are one number seen twice ([ADR 0006](adr/0006-map-visual-quality.md)) | accepted | `T-216`'s own new clause, and the failure it exists to catch is not one oversized panel but four honest ones adding up, which is what the comparison found and what no per-surface rule would have reported. `coveredShare` computes the *union* of the chrome rectangles clipped to the field — a sum would report more than the whole field once two surfaces overlap — and `capture_mockups.ts` calls the same function over the mockups' own surfaces, so the bound is read off the reference by the instrument that measures the build and prints beside every reference capture. The approved set measures 2.7 % / 19.8 % at `full`, 10.3 % / 6.4 % at `compact` and 8.8 % at `stack`; `full` and `stack` are asserted at that measurement rounded up and this build is well inside both. **`compact` is not, and the reason is recorded rather than smoothed over.** At that tier the drawer floats *over* the field instead of taking a slice out of it, and the chrome's rectangles are what `placeOrbit` refuses cards against: bounding those surfaces to 14.4 % of the field placed **three** cards at 1440×900 where D-193 recorded two. `T-216` requires the recorded numbers not to change, so the bound is set above this build's worst, the gap to the reference is a finding in `SPEC.md` §17, and the trade is stated for the acceptance to take or refuse — the reference's 10.3 % costs the 2 / 6 the gate holds |
| D-202 | The `T-216` captures are **accepted**: `T-215` and `T-216` close, `T-210` closes with them, R21 closes, and Phase 3 is unblocked — and the `compact` tier's chrome bound is accepted as a ratchet rather than as the reference's own share ([ADR 0006](adr/0006-map-visual-quality.md)) | accepted | The decision the whole of Phase 2.1 existed to make possible, made the way ADR 0006 clause 5 says it must be: by a person looking at two sets of pictures. D-196 was the same clause exercised in the other direction six days' work earlier, which is what makes this one worth recording — the gate was green both times, and only one of the two answers was yes. What is accepted is the composition **and** the four differences `SPEC.md` §17 records as remaining, each of them measured rather than described: the `compact` chrome bound, Explore's search float carrying the outline's trigger and the companion sentence where the reference draws a one-line chip, ten automatic labels at the review viewport against the reference's eight, and a Focus background drawn at the camera's framing rather than the graph's. **The first of those was a trade rather than an explanation, and it was taken deliberately.** The chrome's rectangles are what `placeOrbit` refuses cards against, so the share and the card counts are one number seen twice: bounding those surfaces to the reference's 10.3 % was measured to place three cards at 1440×900 where D-193 recorded two, and the alternative on the table was a sixth child of `T-210` that tightened the bound and re-measured the regression surface with it. The recorded numbers stand and the bound is a ratchet, so a future task may still take that trade — what it may not do is take it silently, because D-201 now asserts the bound and D-193's numbers are what it would move |
| D-203 | **A green suite is evidence about what it looks at.** Seventy-one findings from a six-reader cross-cutting audit of a tree whose every gate was already passing — 2,533 Python tests, 707 frontend tests, ruff, mypy and `tsc --strict` all clean — are closed here, and the shape they share is the decision worth recording rather than any one of them | accepted | Every guard that missed one of these was real, and every one was pointed at the half of its problem that was already known. The stylesheet suite asserted that the reduced-motion block exists and that every box property is logical, and never computed a **number** — so two tokens sat below WCAG AA while 707 tests stayed green. `validate_coverage` measured a window against a bound the *audited document* supplied, so a bundle could name the ruler it was measured against and claim `PASS` over 29 of 30 unaudited minutes. The `ui` extra was checked against the packages it **declares**, so an undeclared `starlette` import was invisible to the one job built to catch exactly that. `params.py` asserted in prose that `tests/test_api_contract` compares the served document against the frozen one; no such comparison existed, and writing it found a second one — a closed vocabulary the route took as a bare string. Six of sixteen visual scenarios asserted their acceptance numbers only on a machine holding the private library. `mapStyle.clear()` was called only in test setup, by the four suites that need it *because* the singleton carries state across mounts. And the map README stated three retired numbers a reader would have taken from the spec.

So the fixes are guards at the level the misses happened, not repairs: 53 contrast assertions over both palettes; a frontend **lint gate** (D-114's asymmetry, mirrored — nine `react-hooks/exhaustive-deps` suppressions were suppressing nothing) and a **type-check program for the capture scripts** (D-156's defect, reopened by the files added after it, and it found an unhandled rejection on its first run), both CI steps; a parameter-by-parameter drift comparison between the served and frozen documents; a `PagedList` that is the one place the error/loading/empty/total/items/More ladder is decided, because five copies were five copies of every bug in it; `import.meta.glob` guards that read *every* component rather than a list, because a list is what went stale; and citation guards over the numbers prose quotes. **One finding's answer was found by measuring it rather than by reading it**: the card reservations were reported as never enforced, and enforcing them was tried and reverted — over all 86 centres of the real library, 63 lay a card out taller than its reservation and *none* of them overlaps, so the box is a seating input and the sentence calling it an upper bound over the drawn card was the actual defect. True upper bounds cost a recorded card (7 / 1 becomes 6 / 2) to buy nothing a reader sees, so the numbers stand, the claim is corrected where it was written, and the gate asserts the invariant a reader would notice — no two cards over the same pixels — over ten centres rather than two. Two defects were found by the new tests rather than by the audit: an infinite loop in the markdown parser on a line that starts like a fence and is not one, and a fourth unmirrored `→`. Every CI action is pinned to a commit with the version beside it, and `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md` is walked from the lockfile's production closure rather than from a list. **What is not fixed is named**: SPEC §5's `stack` tier turned out to be *delivered* and the stylesheet to contradict itself about who owed it, and the recorded acceptance numbers still cannot be asserted without the private library — that skip is now stated in the report and `X2KNWLDG_BROWSER_REQUIRE_RECORDED=1` makes it a failure where the library serves the mockups' own centre |

| D-204 | Insert Phase 2.2 / `T-220` before Canvas and qualify a no-payment Twitter/X acquisition path on the user's real Iran environment before integration ([ADR 0007](adr/0007-twitter-acquisition-boundary.md)) | accepted | The user cannot currently pay for the official API and explicitly chose the Treasury-style/local problem to solve. `x-cli` remains the primary candidate because it is local and emits structured public data, but it is young, AGPL-3.0 and not yet proved in-region, so `T-222` measures it before dependency or implementation. FxTwitter/FxEmbed is explicit opt-in fallback because the post id and request metadata leave the machine; official oEmbed is corroboration only; Firefox fallback passively imports responses normal browsing already received and never stores a session. Treasury/twscrape account pools, passwords, rotation, browser automation and evasion are excluded. Every route ends at one provider-neutral immutable capture with honest completeness. Canvas is technically unblocked but deliberately deferred until this phase gate closes |
| D-210 | Persian/RTL text is **not damaged** by any qualified route, and the routes do **not** disagree about the authored text either; entity spans are **codepoint** offsets taken from the provider's facets | accepted, superseding this row's own first version | Measured by [`fidelity.py`](spikes/T-222/fidelity.py) over four Persian posts from three accounts. ZWNJ (`U+200C`), Persian ye (`U+06CC`), keheh (`U+06A9`) and Persian digits came back identical every time — and ZWNJ is not marginal, appearing in **53 of 60** posts sampled from one Persian account, so folding it would have corrupted most of the corpus. **The first version of this row said the routes disagree about link representation and agree only after URL normalization. That was a field-selection error on my part**: I compared FxTwitter's `tweet.text`, a *rendered* field with links expanded and a trailing media link dropped. Its `raw_text.text` is the authored form, and on that field the routes are byte-identical on all four posts, media and links included, so the corroboration check needs no normalization and raises no false positives. Two further requirements came out of it. Entities must come from `raw_text.facets`, which carries `indices`/`original`/`replacement` as a triple: x-cli's own `entities.urls` holds expanded URLs that appear nowhere in the text, and one post had two `t.co` links against one entry, so pairing from that field is a guess. And the span basis is **codepoints, proven against astral emoji** — on a post carrying 📺 and 📡 before its link the codepoint slice returns the authored URL exactly while the UTF-16 slice is mangled and shifted by two, so a capture built on the UTF-16 reading would corrupt every span after the first emoji, in precisely the posts a Persian corpus is full of |
| D-212 | The Twitter capture contract lives in **`schemas/capture/v1/`**, is **self-contained**, and is shaped so every dishonest record is *unrepresentable* rather than discouraged | accepted | Placement first: it is a pipeline contract upstream of extraction, so it does not reference `schemas/v1/` — that layer is derived *from* captures and documents itself as describing "nothing that the pipeline writes", so the dependency would run backwards. Every primitive is duplicated into its own `$defs` deliberately, even where a near-twin exists. The design rule is that a schema accepting an honest document proves little, so each constraint was chosen against a specific lie T-222 measured as possible: `tier` omits 2 so a session capture cannot be expressed at all; `completeness.downward` has no `complete`; `anchor.terminal_claim` has no `observed`; `media`/`edits` carry `minItems: 1` and `poll`/`article` `minProperties: 1`, so `[]` and `{}` cannot claim absence was *observed*; `metrics` requires `observed_at`; `sha256_raw` sits beside `sha256_sanitized`. The twelve-entry rejection catalogue in `tests/test_twitter_capture.py` is the real test of the contract. **One finding handed to `T-228`:** `schemas/v1/locator.schema.json` already reserves a `post_id` branch and a `text_span` branch, but neither carries both, and a Twitter claim needs a post id *plus* a span. Adding the span fields to the `post_id` branch is additive and so an in-place `v1` edit, but that file belongs to the index layer and `T-228` owns it — it is not touched here |
| D-211 | The **canonical capture text is the authored form**, `t.co` links included; expanded link targets are stored as **text entities beside the span**, never substituted into it | accepted (user, 2026-09-03) | A source claim cites a post id plus an exact text span, so span offsets are offsets into whichever form is canonical and the choice binds every locator in the corpus. The authored form wins on three grounds measured in D-210: it is what the author wrote and what raw evidence contains; the default route (x-cli Tier 1) returns it natively, so no third party is needed to build a capture; and expansion is a lossy provider opinion that can lengthen the text — one `t.co` expanded to a longer `x.com/i/broadcasts/…` URL — so adopting it would let a provider policy change shift every span in a stored corpus without the post changing at all. The rejected alternatives: expanded-as-canonical, which reads better in the Reader but makes the contract depend on the opt-in third party and on its stability; and storing no expansion, which is smallest but leaves a reader unable to tell where a link goes offline, and `t.co` may outlive neither the post nor the service |
| D-209 | The user's **always-on tunnel is part of the target environment** and becomes a named dependency of Phase 2.2; `T-222`'s measurements therefore describe the real environment | accepted (user confirmed 2026-09-03) | Verified after the fact and independently: `ipinfo.io` reported `country: US, region: New York`, `cloudflare.com/cdn-cgi/trace` reported `loc=US, colo=EWR, ip=50.117.3.112`, and `utun4` was active at `10.141.112.250 --> 10.141.0.1`. Shell proxy variables were unset and the macOS system proxy was off, which is why the tunnel escaped both checks — the premise had been taken from the user's stated location rather than measured. Capability findings are unaffected, being properties of X's surfaces and of the tool. Reachability from Iran, the latency table and the rate-limit budgets are affected, the last because X budgets per IP and a shared egress may share them. The user confirmed the tunnel is always on, which resolves it: the measurements describe the target environment. Follow-up measurements then removed the confound — the egress is stable (`50.117.3.112`, `colo=EWR`, three samples, no rotation) and `limits.json` consumption matches this session's own requests, so budgets behave as a dedicated egress rather than a shared one. Two obligations follow: `T-223`/`T-224` must distinguish "tunnel down" from "provider changed", since a routine network drop must not read as provider drift and discard a good capture; and ADR 0007's "no payment or regional restriction is to be circumvented" — written about not evading the paid API — needs one clarifying line, because routing around a state-level block on one's own traffic is not evading X's access controls or payment. Reachability from a bare Iranian egress stays unmeasured and unclaimed |
| D-205 | `T-222` qualifies a credential-free Twitter/X acquisition path from the user's real Iran environment: **`GO`**, with `tamnd/x-cli` v0.5.0 pinned as an externally installed AGPL-3.0 binary ([spike report](spikes/T-222/REPORT.md)) | accepted for the capability half; environment half **superseded by D-209** | Measured, not read: 52 cells across four routes, 0 `FAIL`, every Tier 0 surface reachable without payment — though from a US tunnel egress rather than an Iranian one, see D-209. Public single posts qualify on three independent routes. The measurement also contradicted the candidate's own field table in four places, so `T-223` must freeze on observation. Nothing was integrated; the tool stays a replaceable subprocess and the Python core keeps zero dependencies |
| D-206 | A same-author self-thread is **complete only when its anchor is the thread's deepest post**. **Chosen contract (user, 2026-09-03): ingestion asks for the thread's last post and reports `PASS`; a root anchor warns and asks for the last post instead.** Completeness is recorded as *"complete to root from a user-asserted terminal anchor"* — never as an observed fact | accepted | Upward is provable — following `reply_to` terminates at a parent-less root, all hops resolved and single-author, demonstrated over a real 10-post thread at Tier 0 with no credential. Downward is not: `x thread` and `x replies` return only the anchor at every credential-free tier (`n=1` against 11,337 and 18,011 replies, across three surfaces), the whole reply tree is Tier 2, and a 250-post author archive held **3 of 10** members — the seven absent posts being exactly what a naive implementation would drop while reporting success. Of the three options the user chose asking for the last post, which keeps `PASS` meaningful instead of making `PARTIAL` the normal state, and declined promoting `T-226` — reasonably, since it is untested and probably inherits the same wall, "I scrolled to the end" being as unprovable as "this is the last post". The residual risk is explicit and permanent: the terminal anchor is a human judgement the system cannot verify, so a thread continued later will be silently short. `T-226` remains optional |
| D-207 | Make x-cli **Tier 1 (anonymous guest token)** the default capture read rather than Tier 0 | accepted (user, 2026-09-03) | Tier 0 is the most conservative route and the least honest one for long posts: on a real 2967-character post it returned **280 characters — 9%** — cut mid-sentence, and carried no `is_note_tweet`, `truncated` or `note_tweet` field, so truncation is undetectable in band and a complete short post is indistinguishable from a truncated long one. Tier 1 passed **every** MVP cell (13/13) and agreed with FxTwitter character-for-character at 521 and 2967. A guest token is an anonymous value X mints on request, bound to no account, stored as `guest_token` + `minted_at`, budget 500/15 min — it is not a credential, session or account, so it sits inside ADR 0007's exclusions, but the ADR did not name it and it is an escalation from Tier 0, so it needed explicit ratification rather than assumption, and has it. Tier 0 remains the fallback for short single posts and for the parent-walk, where it is unmetered and strongest |
| D-208 | The qualified artefact is x-cli **v0.5.0**, AGPL-3.0, invoked as a separately installed pinned binary at **`~/.local/bin/x`**, with its recorded digest verified at runtime | accepted (user, 2026-09-03) | Tarball SHA-256 `6de9cde4…a705` verified against the release `checksums.txt`; extracted binary SHA-256 `6cb6b7f9…7c97`. Maintenance risk stated rather than hidden: the repository was created 2026-06-13, last pushed 2026-07-29, has 15 stars and **one contributor**, so it is qualified as a tool we invoke and can replace, never as a library we depend on. Installed and verified on 2026-09-03: `~/.local/bin/x` reports `x 0.5.0 (commit ff9aa9e, built 2026-07-29T02:41:51Z, darwin/arm64)` and its SHA-256 matches the recorded pin. `~/.local/bin` is on `PATH`, outside the repository, so nothing is vendored and the AGPL boundary holds. `T-224` must refuse a version or digest mismatch rather than run whatever `x` it finds |
| D-213 | The provider seam **hashes the binary before it executes it**, never searches `PATH`, and can reach only two subcommands | accepted | Three refusals rather than three preferences, each closing a way the seam could have been talked into running the wrong thing. **Digest first:** `verify` computes the SHA-256 of the file on disk and compares it with D-208's pin *before* `x version` is ever spawned, so a binary that is not the pinned build is refused without being run — the check that would catch an unexpected binary must not be the check that runs it, and `test_a_digest_mismatch_is_refused_without_running_the_binary` proves it with a stub that would leave a sentinel file behind. **No `PATH`:** the path comes from an explicit argument, else `X2KNWLDG_XCLI`, else `~/.local/bin/x`, which is what makes D-208's "refuse a mismatch rather than run whatever `x` it finds" true rather than intended; the override moves *where* to look and cannot move *what is acceptable*, because the digest is still checked. **An allowlist of `{tweet, version}`:** `x auth import` is the tool's browser-cookie reader, and an allowlist makes it unreachable from this package by construction rather than by nobody having called it (ADR 0007 invariant 1). Alongside: `--no-cache` on every read, so no capture rests on the tool's HTTP cache instead of a request; the post id is its own `argv` element with no shell anywhere; output is bounded and an over-limit response is **refused rather than truncated**, because half a JSON document is not evidence; and a timeout kills the child rather than leaving it. The capture's `provider` block is *observed* — the digest by hashing, the version string by asking — and only then compared with the pin, so the provenance is evidence about the binary rather than a restatement of our own expectation |
| D-214 | **Exit 8 is x-cli's whole transport class, not "timeout"**, and a rate limit is transient rather than provider drift. Three new CLI exit codes carry the distinction to the shell | accepted | Measured on 2026-09-04, correcting the [spike report](spikes/T-222/REPORT.md) §6 table, which listed `8` as timeout alone: `HTTPS_PROXY=http://127.0.0.1:1 x tweet <id> --tier guest --no-cache -o json` exits **8** with `Cannot reach x.com: proxyconnect tcp: dial tcp 127.0.0.1:1: connect: connection refused`. So the exit status does not separate a dropped tunnel from a slow request, and the message does; both are classified as transport and **neither is a fact about the provider**, which is D-209's obligation. A transient failure aborts with **nothing written** — no capture, no evidence, no coverage verdict — because a dropped tunnel part way up a thread would otherwise produce a `PARTIAL` indistinguishable from a thread that genuinely ends there. Exit `5` (rate limited) joins that class rather than being reported as drift: the tool did exactly what it should, and the budget is a property of X rather than of the tool's output — T-222 measured the guest tier metering per operation, so a thread walk and an archive read draw on different budgets. At the shell: **`7`** the pinned provider is missing or is not the pin (an install or a deliberate re-pin), **`8`** the read could not be completed and nothing was learned (tunnel down, timeout, or rate limited — the stderr envelope names which), **`9`** the provider answered and the answer was unusable. `8` and `9` are different codes because a caller that cannot tell them apart will either retry a real drift forever or discard a good path over the network's behaviour. Coverage verdicts still leave through `0`/`3`/`4`, so a `PARTIAL` thread cannot be read as a whole one |
| D-215 | `network.via_tunnel` is **stated by the operator, never inferred**, and `capture` refuses to run without a statement | accepted | The schema requires the field and offers no "unknown", and this command genuinely cannot measure it: a `utun` interface existing does not prove traffic routes through it, and asking a third party for the egress address would be a network request made in order to describe a network request. D-209 exists precisely because an environment premise was *taken* rather than established, so the seam does not repeat the mistake in code — `--via-tunnel` / `--no-tunnel`, or a standing `X2KNWLDG_VIA_TUNNEL=1/0` in a shell profile, and otherwise a refusal naming D-209. The refusal is evaluated **before** the provider is verified, so declining to guess never costs a request. `--tunnel-note` records the basis beside it |
| D-216 | An unavailable post's raw evidence is **the tool's own message**, and an unavailability with no message is drift | accepted | The contract requires at least one `raw_evidence` entry per capture, and rightly: a finding with no preserved bytes is an assertion. A `not_found` read produces no stdout, so what was actually observed is the tool's stderr — `Tweet not found: <id> (deleted, suspended, or protected).` — and that is what is preserved, sanitized and digested like any other evidence, at `raw/<route>_<id>.unavailable.txt`. The capture still names none of the three reasons: they are one message below Tier 2 (D-205), so `availability.reason` stays `not_determinable_at_this_tier`. If a future build reports an unavailability with no message at all, there is nothing to cite and the seam raises drift rather than writing a `FAIL` that rests on nothing |
| D-217 | The **root-first thread invariant is conditional on upward completeness**, which is a `T-223` refinement `T-224` forced | accepted | `test_thread_order_is_root_first_and_parent_consistent` asserted unconditionally that a `parent_links` capture's first item carries no `parent_post_id`. That is true of every committed fixture and false of an honestly truncated chain: a walk that stops at an unavailable parent, or at a parent by **another author** (outside the MVP per ADR 0007), does not begin at a root, and its first item keeps the `parent_post_id` that proves it. Dropping that link to satisfy a root-first rule would hide the very incompleteness the capture is reporting, so the rule moved instead: the root claim now applies when `completeness.upward.status` is `complete`, and where the chain dangles the dangling end **must equal** the id `upward.unresolved_at` names — a stronger statement than the one it replaces, since it ties the item set and the completeness claim together. The eight committed captures are unaffected and still pass |
| D-218 | At the qualified local route a capture carries **mention spans and no URL spans**, and that is a property of the route | accepted | Measured live on 2026-09-04. x-cli's `entities.urls` holds *expanded* URLs that appear nowhere in the authored text, so a `t.co` link can be located in the text but not paired with what it points at: one post of the NASA thread carried `https://t.co/ZKTzQCAGxC https://t.co/IOuHw9MYwr` against the single entry `https://go.nasa.gov/4chzg9c`, reproducing the pairing failure T-222 described. A span whose target is guessed is worse than no span, and the facet triple that makes the pairing safe comes from the opt-in route `T-225` owns. Mentions are different and do work: the tool lists the handles, the span is located in the text, and only an unambiguous single occurrence is recorded — verified live, 7 of the 10 thread posts carrying mention spans that re-slice to their own handle. Absent, not wrong; `T-225` is what adds URL spans, and `T-227` decides whether it needs them |
| D-219 | The record→capture normalization has **one implementation**, in the package, and `T-223`'s fixture builder imports it | accepted | `post_from`, `entities_from` and `mentions_from` were written for the `T-223` fixtures and the seam needs exactly them; two copies of "how a provider record becomes a capture" would be two answers the moment one was edited, and the codepoint-offset rule and the absent-not-empty rule are precisely the kind that drift silently. They moved to `src/x2knwldg/twitter/normalize.py` unchanged and `tests/fixtures/captures/build_captures.py` now imports them over `sys.path`, the way `tests/fixtures/runs/build_fixtures.py` already imports the pipeline. The move needed no new test to be trusted: `T-223`'s byte-identical regeneration check *is* the proof, and all eight committed captures still rebuild to the same bytes |
| D-220 | A source claim cites a **post id and a text span**; URL entity spans are not a prerequisite, so `T-227` runs before `T-225` | accepted | The `T-227` row already names the citation unit in as many words — "a source claim cites a post id and exact text span/excerpt" — and D-218 measured that the qualified local route yields mention spans and **no** URL spans, because `x-cli`'s `entities.urls` holds expanded targets that appear nowhere in the authored text. Making URL spans a prerequisite would put an **explicit opt-in network route** on the critical path of the default local one, inverting the provider order [ADR 0007](adr/0007-twitter-acquisition-boundary.md) approved, where FxTwitter is opt-in and oEmbed is corroborative. The contract already absorbs the later addition: `text.entities` is optional and `textEntity.kind` already accepts `url`, so `T-225` adds spans additively — no capture is re-acquired, no locator changes basis, and no `schemas/capture/v2/`. The cost is recorded rather than hidden: until `T-225` exists a claim about a link cites the span of the `t.co` text **as authored** (D-211), and every capture extraction consumes carries `text.completeness.status: unverified` |
| D-221 | The **dangling-chain capture has no fixture**, and D-217's refinement is therefore unexercised — `T-227`'s partial-thread case builds one | accepted | `test_thread_order_is_root_first_and_parent_consistent` grew an `else` branch for the chain that honestly does not begin at a root, and **no committed capture reaches it**: it returns early unless `order.basis` is `parent_links` with two or more items, and the only capture meeting that is `pass-thread-terminal-anchor`, which is `upward: complete`. `partial-thread-root-anchor` is `PARTIAL` for the *other* reason — a root anchor whose descendants are unenumerable — and is a single item. So the one assertion that ties the item set to the completeness claim has been dead against the fixture set since D-217 was written. It is buildable from evidence already committed: `docs/spikes/T-222/fixtures/` confirms `2094033367767339277`'s parent is `2094028067924504596`, both in the NASA thread, so `MANIFEST.json[7:]` plus a `not_found` read for that parent yields a three-item `PARTIAL` whose first item keeps the `parent_post_id` that `upward.unresolved_at` names. This is also the tombstone-inside-a-thread shape: `twitter.acquire._walk_upward` stops at an unavailable parent rather than including it, so a missing member is an omitted item with a reason, never an unavailable item mid-chain. **Amended on building it: the construction above is a false statement about a real post and was not committed.** `2094028067924504596` is *available* — its successful response is committed as `raw/nasa_roman_2094028067924504596.json` — so a read recording it as `not_found` asserts something measurement contradicts three files away. The mechanical tests would not catch it, which makes it worse rather than better: a `not_found` read carries its message inline in `routes_read[].error_text` and needs no `raw_evidence` entry at all, as `fail-unavailable-post` shows. The alternatives were checked and each fails for its own reason: `999999999999999999` is genuinely unavailable, but making it the dangling parent means falsifying `items[0].parent_post_id`, which is the very field D-217's test compares against `upward.unresolved_at`; and the crossed-author form of the same shape — which needs no unavailability at all, since `_walk_upward` stops at a parent by another author — needs a post whose parent is by someone else, and every thread record committed anywhere in this repository is NASA replying to NASA. **Measured live instead, on 2026-09-03, and committed as `partial-thread-dangling-chain`.** The *crossed-author* form needs no unavailability at all, so nothing had to be faked: two `ylecun` posts walked from the last one, stopping at a parent by another author, which ADR 0007 puts outside the MVP. Finding one took a sweep of 17 accounts and roughly 680 records for a single hit, which is a measurement in its own right — the shape is common in reality and *rare on the credential-free surface*, because the guest timeline returns a ranked selection (REPORT.md §4) that favours originals and complete self-threads. `x replies <user>` returned 40 records with **no** `reply_to` at all. The capture the seam wrote and the committed fixture were compared field by field and are equal apart from `raw_evidence[].path`, which must differ because a run directory is gitignored; no test can re-check that, so it is recorded rather than asserted. The crossed parent's bytes are not in the repository: `_walk_upward` stops before appending it, so `_preserve_reads` never sees it and only its id appears. `test_some_fixture_exercises_the_dangling_chain_branch` now fails if the branch goes dead again |
| D-222 | The **edit case is covered by a capture labelled synthetic**, because no measured route produces one | accepted | The `T-227` row's acceptance clause names an `edit/tombstone` case and [the spike report](spikes/T-222/REPORT.md) §12 measured edited posts as `NOT_SUPPORTED` — no instance in the 610-post credential-free scan, and `x fields tweet` declares `edits` with no surface at any tier. There is no raw evidence in this repository from which an `edits` capture can be built, and the three ways out are not equal: a refusal-catalogue entry leaves no canonical output for the shape, and narrowing the clause to tombstone would make `T-229`'s capability table say edits are *unhandled* when the contract can represent them. So one capture carries `edits` and is marked synthetic the way the run fixtures are (`"fixture": true` with a `fixture_note`, the `T-006` pattern), with the note saying no route produced it. What it pins is behaviour, not a measurement: extraction cites only `text.canonical`, names the prior ids as omitted, and **never fetches them** — so if `edits` ever arrives on a real capture, the handling is already decided rather than improvised against live data. **Amended on building it: the label is unrepresentable and the fixture cannot live in `tests/fixtures/captures/`.** The capture schema's root is `additionalProperties: false`, so `"fixture": true` has nowhere to go — and there is no honest field to borrow, because `network.note` and `order.note` are about the network and the ordering. Worse, `test_a_capture_can_be_revalidated_from_raw_evidence` is parametrized over *every* file in that directory and requires each `raw_evidence` digest to recompute from committed bytes, so a synthetic capture needs synthetic committed bytes attributed to the pinned provider at a real tier with a real `request_shape` — a capture asserting the provider returned what it never returned, which is the class of lie the `sha256_raw`/`sha256_sanitized` pair and `additionalProperties: false` exist to prevent. The two fixture directories have **opposite** contracts and this is what separates them: `tests/fixtures/captures/README.md` opens by declaring every fixture derived from "the bytes a real acquisition returned", while `tests/fixtures/runs/README.md` opens in bold with "Nothing in this directory is real evidence". One exception would make the first sentence false for all nine captures. So the `edits` capture is constructed **in-test**, and its *extraction outputs* are a committed fixture in `T-227`'s run tree, where `"fixture": true` in `metadata.json` is the established `T-006` pattern — a pipeline output is not evidence |
| D-223 | `downward.reason` is conditioned on the **upward claim** as well as the anchor's role, and a cross-field test guards the prose | accepted | Found by acquiring D-221's chain live. `_assemble` keyed the reason on `role` alone, so a `thread_terminal` anchor whose walk stopped at another author's post still read *"complete to root from a user-asserted terminal anchor"* — two fields away from an `upward.status` of `incomplete` with basis `unresolved_hop` saying it did not. One record, two contradictory statements, and **both halves validate**: `reason` is free text and JSON Schema compares no two fields, so the twelve-entry rejection catalogue could not have caught it either. This is the same class as D-217 and the mirror of it: D-217 made the *item set* conditional on upward completeness and left the *prose* unconditional.
